杜·克鲁瓦西耶指望把德·埃斯格里尼翁家逼到穷途末路；他指望看到他们的城堡拆毁，土地被零星拍卖，全靠这个轻狂少年几乎必然要闯出的祸事。他的盘算到此为止；他不像院长迪·龙瑟雷那样，想到维克图尼安可能给司法提供另一种把柄。两人都在维克图尼安狂妄的虚荣和贪图享乐里，为他们复仇的计划找到了盟友。院长迪·龙瑟雷的儿子，一个17岁的小伙子，担当教唆者的角色再合适不过。他是伯爵的伙伴之一，是拿杜·克鲁瓦西耶薪金的新型密探；杜·克鲁瓦西耶向他传授课业，派他去利用那个高贵而漂亮的孩子的优点跟踪他，又冷笑着唆使他去助长受难者最坏的恶习。法比安·迪·龙瑟雷是个心眼很活的少年，这种捉弄人的勾当正对他胃口；他恰好有那种精明的头脑和嫉恨的天性，能在这样一场追逐里，找到心思灵巧的人在外省所欠缺的那种醉心的消遣。
从18岁到21岁的3年里，维克图尼安让可怜的谢内尔花了将近8万法郎！而这一切，阿尔芒德小姐和侯爵都蒙在鼓里。这笔钱一半以上用于买通官司；余下的被这少年的挥霍糟蹋光了。侯爵1万里弗尔的年金中，5000是家用必需；还有2000是阿尔芒德小姐的私房（尽管她节俭）和侯爵的开销。因此，这位英俊的假定继承人手头能花的不到100路易。2000里弗尔能撑起什么样的场面呢？维克图尼安单是裁缝的账单就吞掉了他的全部零用。他的内衣、服装、手套和香水都从巴黎来。他还想要一匹上等的英国鞍马、一辆双轮轻马车和一匹副马。杜·克鲁瓦西耶先生有一辆双轮轻马车和一匹纯种马。难道让资产阶级压过贵族不成？再者，年轻的伯爵还得雇一个穿埃斯格里尼翁家号衣的仆人。他自豪于在城里和全郡的青年中开风气之先；他踏进了那个与青春、美貌和才智如此相称的奢华与考究的世界。

===Original===

Chesnel paid for it all, not without using, like ancient parliaments, the right of protest, albeit he spoke with angelic kindness.
“What a pity it is that so good a man should be so tiresome!” Victurnien would say to himself every time that the notary staunched some wound in his purse.
Chesnel had been left a widower, and childless; he had taken his old master’s son to fill the void in his heart. It was a pleasure to him to watch the lad driving up the High Street, perched aloft on the box-seat of the tilbury, whip in hand, and a rose in his buttonhole, handsome, well turned out, envied by everyone.
Pressing need would bring Victurnien with uneasy eyes and coaxing manner, but steady voice, to the modest house in the Rue du Bercail; there had been losses at cards at the Troisvilles, or the Duc de Verneuil’s, or the prefecture, or the receiver-general’s, and the Count had come to his providence, the notary. He had only to show himself to carry the day.
“Well, what is it, M. le Comte? What has happened?” the old man would ask, with a tremor in his voice.
On great occasions Victurnien would sit down, assume a melancholy, pensive expression, and submit with little coquetries of voice and gesture to be questioned. Then when he had thoroughly roused the old man’s fears (for Chesnel was beginning to fear how such a course of extravagance would end), he would own up to a peccadillo which a bill for a thousand francs would absolve. Chesnel possessed a private income of some twelve thousand livres, but the fund was not inexhaustible. The eighty thousand francs thus squandered represented his savings, accumulated for the day when the Marquis should send his son to Paris, or open negotiations for a wealthy marriage.
Chesnel was clear-sighted so long as Victurnien was not there before him. One by one he lost the illusions which the Marquis and his sister still fondly cherished. He saw that the young fellow could not be depended upon in the least, and wished to see him married to some modest, sensible girl of good birth, wondering within himself how a young man could mean so well and do so ill, for he made promises one day only to break them all on the next.
But there is never any good to be expected of young men who confess their sins and repent, and straightway fall into them again.

===Chinese===

谢内尔付了这一切账，并非不使用——像古代的议会那样——抗议的权利，只是他说得天使般和气。
“这么好的人偏偏这么麻烦，多可惜啊！”每当公证人替他堵住钱袋上的窟窿，维克图尼安总要这样自言自语。
谢内尔丧妻无子；他把老主人的儿子抱来填补心中的空缺。看着这少年赶着双轮轻马车驶上大街，高高地坐在车夫座上，手执马鞭，钮扣眼里插着一朵玫瑰，英俊，神气，人人艳羡，他心里就高兴。
手头一紧，维克图尼安便带着不安的眼神和哄人的做派——嗓音却是稳的——来到贝卡尔街那所朴素的房子；或是特鲁瓦维尔家、韦尔讷伊公爵家、省长官邸或总税务官家里牌桌上输了钱，伯爵便来投奔他的救星公证人。他只消露一露面，事情就成。
“怎么样，伯爵先生？出什么事了？”老人问，声音里带着颤抖。
逢到重大的关头，维克图尼安便坐下，摆出忧郁沉思的表情，任凭人家盘问，还拿腔拿调、比比画画地卖弄一番。等把老人的恐惧吊足了（谢内尔已经开始害怕这样挥霍下去会是什么结局），他才招认一桩小小的过失，一张1000法郎的账单便可赦免。谢内尔自己有大约1.2万里弗尔的进项，可家底并非取之不尽。这样挥霍掉的8万法郎，是他多年积攒的储蓄，原打算留给侯爵送儿子去巴黎、或为儿子议一门有钱亲事的那一天。
只要维克图尼安不在眼前，谢内尔是明察的。侯爵和他妹妹还痴痴怀着的幻想，他一个个丢掉了。他看出这小伙子半点靠不住，想让他娶一个门第好、头脑清楚、性子温厚的姑娘；他心里纳闷，一个年轻人怎么能存心那么好、行事却那么糟——他今天许下愿，明天便统统忘掉。
可是，凡是认罪悔改、转身又立刻故态复萌的年轻人，永远别指望有什么好处。

===Original===

A man of strong character only confesses his faults to himself, and punishes himself for them; as for the weak, they drop back into the old ruts when they find that the bank is too steep to climb. The springs of pride which lie in a great man’s secret soul had been slackened in Victurnien. With such guardians as he had, such company as he kept, such a life as he led, he had suddenly became an enervated voluptuary at that turning-point in his life when a man most stands in need of the harsh discipline of misfortune and adversity which formed a Prince Eugène, a Frederick II, a Napoleon. Chesnel saw that Victurnien possessed that uncontrollable appetite for enjoyments which should be the prerogative of men endowed with giant powers; the men who feel the need of counterbalancing their gigantic labors by pleasures which bring one-sided mortals to the pit.
At times the good man stood aghast; then, again, some profound sally, some sign of the lad’s remarkable range of intellect, would reassure him. He would say, as the Marquis said at the rumor of some escapade, “Boys will be boys.” Chesnel had spoken to the Chevalier, lamenting the young lord’s propensity for getting into debt; but the Chevalier manipulated his pinch of snuff, and listened with a smile of amusement.
“My dear Chesnel, just explain to me what a national debt is,” he answered. “If France has debts, egad! why should not Victurnien have debts? At this time and at all times princes have debts, every gentleman has debts. Perhaps you would rather that Victurnien should bring you his savings?﻿—Do you know that our great Richelieu (not the Cardinal, a pitiful fellow that put nobles to death, but the Maréchal), do you know what he did once when his grandson the Prince de Chinon, the last of the line, let him see that he had not spent his pocket-money at the University?”
“No, M. le Chevalier.”
“Oh, well; he flung the purse out of the window to a sweeper in the courtyard, and said to his grandson, ‘Then they do not teach you to be a prince here?’ ”
Chesnel bent his head and made no answer. But that night, as he lay awake, he thought that such doctrines as these were fatal in times when there was one law for everybody, and foresaw the first beginnings of the ruin of the d’Esgrignons.

===Chinese===

性格坚强的人只向自己认错，并且自己惩罚自己；至于软弱的人，一见河岸太陡爬不上去，便重新滚回旧车辙里去了。伟人秘藏的灵魂里那些骄傲的弹簧，在维克图尼安身上早已松了劲。有这样的监护人，有这样的伙伴，过着这样的生活，他在人生最需要不幸与逆境的严酷磨炼的转折点上——那种磨炼造就了欧根亲王、腓特烈二世、拿破仑——突然变成了一个委靡的享乐主义者。谢内尔看出，维克图尼安身上有那种克制不住的享乐欲，而那本该是天赋神力之人的特权；那些人感到需要以欢乐来平衡他们巨人般的劳作——正是这些欢乐，把偏至的凡人拖向深渊。
善良的老人时而骇然失色；时而这少年一句深刻的话、一点智力出众的表现，又让他放下心来。他会像侯爵听到某种荒唐事的传闻时那样说：“男孩子终归是男孩子。”谢内尔同骑士先生谈过，哀叹小主人爱借债的习性；骑士先生却捻着一小撮鼻烟，含笑听着。
“亲爱的谢内尔，先给我解释解释什么叫国债，”他答道，“法兰西既然有债，嘿！维克图尼安为什么不能有债？如今也罢，历来也罢，亲王们有债，每个绅士都有债。莫非你倒愿意维克图尼安把省下的钱给你送来？——你可知道我们伟大的黎塞留（不是那位红衣主教，那个杀贵族的可怜虫，是那位元帅）？你可知道，他的外孙希农亲王——家族最后一位——让他看出自己在大学里连零花钱都没花掉时，他干了一件什么事？”
“不知道，骑士先生。”
“哦，是这样：他把钱袋扔出窗外，给了院子里一个扫地的，对外孙说：‘难道他们不教你在这里当亲王吗？’”
谢内尔低下头，没有回答。可是当天夜里，他睡不着，心想在人人一律遵行同一法律的时代，这样的教条是致命的；他预感到德·埃斯格里尼翁家败落的最初征兆。

===Original===

---
But for these explanations which depict one side of provincial life in the time of the Empire and the Restoration, it would not be easy to understand the opening scene of this history, an incident which took place in the great salon one evening towards the end of October 1822. The card-tables were forsaken, the Collection of Antiquities﻿—elderly nobles, elderly countesses, young marquises, and simple baronesses﻿—had settled their losses and winnings. The master of the house was pacing up and down the room, while Mlle. Armande was putting out the candles on the card-tables. He was not taking exercise alone, the Chevalier was with him, and the two wrecks of the eighteenth century were talking of Victurnien. The Chevalier had undertaken to broach the subject with the Marquis.
“Yes, Marquis,” he was saying, “your son is wasting his time and his youth; you ought to send him to court.”
“I have always thought,” said the Marquis, “that if my great age prevents me from going to court﻿—where, between ourselves, I do not know what I should do among all these new people whom his Majesty receives, and all that is going on there﻿—that if I could not go myself, I could at least send my son to present our homage to His Majesty. The King surely would do something for the Count﻿—give him a company, for instance, or a place in the Household, a chance, in short, for the boy to win his spurs. My uncle the Archbishop suffered a cruel martyrdom; I have fought for the cause without deserting the camp with those who thought it their duty to follow the Princes. I held that while the King was in France, his nobles should rally round him.﻿—Ah! well, no one gives us a thought; a Henry IV would have written before now to the d’Esgrignons, ‘Come to me, my friends; we have won the day!’﻿—After all, we are something better than the Troisvilles, yet here are two Troisvilles made peers of France; and another, I hear, represents the nobles in the Chamber.” (He took the upper electoral colleges for assemblies of his own order.) “Really, they think no more of us than if we did not exist. I was waiting for the Princes to make their journey through this part of the world; but as the Princes do not come to us, we must go to the Princes.”
“I am enchanted to learn that you think of introducing our dear Victurnien into society,” the Chevalier put in adroitly.

===Chinese===

---
若不是有了这些描绘帝国与复辟时代外省生活一面的说明，这段历史的开场一幕便不易明白了。那是1822年10月末的一个晚上，事情发生在大客厅里。牌桌已经散了，古物陈列室的诸公——年老的贵族、年老的伯爵夫人、年轻的侯爵夫人和普普通通的男爵夫人们——已经清算了输赢。主人在客厅里踱来踱去，阿尔芒德小姐正在熄灭牌桌上的蜡烛。踱步的不止他一人，骑士先生陪着他，两个十八世纪的遗老正在谈论维克图尼安。骑士先生已经承担了向侯爵开口提起这个话题的任务。
“是啊，侯爵，”他正说着，“您的儿子在浪费他的光阴和青春；您应该送他进宫廷。”
“我一向在想，”侯爵说，“既然我的高龄使我没法进宫廷——说句体己话，在那儿，在陛下接待的这批新人中间，在那边发生的一切当中，我简直不知道自己能做什么——既然我自己去不了，至少可以派我儿子去向陛下致敬。国王准会给这伯爵一点什么——比如说给他一个连队，或在王室内府安排个职位，总之给这孩子一个扬名立万的机会。我的叔祖大主教受过残酷的殉难；我为这个事业战斗过，没有跟着那些自认有义务追随亲王们的人一起逃离阵营。我坚持认为，国王还在法兰西的日子里，他的贵族就该团结在他周围。——啊！算了，没有一个人想到我们；换了亨利四世，早就给埃斯格里尼翁家写信了：‘到我这儿来吧，我的朋友们；我们打赢了！’——说到底，我们总比特鲁瓦维尔家强些吧，可人家两个特鲁瓦维尔当了法兰西贵族院议员；听说还有一位，在议院里代表贵族。”（他把上级选举团当成了他自己等级的议会。）“真的，他们把我们看得仿佛不存在一样。我原在等亲王们驾临这一带；既然亲王们不到我们这儿来，我们就只好到亲王们那儿去了。”
“听说您有意把我们的宝贝维克图尼安引进社交界，我真是太高兴了。”骑士先生乘机巧妙地插了一句。

===Original===

“He ought not to bury his talents in a hole like this town. The best fortune that he can look for here is to come across some Norman girl” (mimicking the accent), “country-bred, stupid, and rich. What could he make of her?﻿—his wife? Oh! good Lord!”
“I sincerely hope that he will defer his marriage until he has obtained some great office or appointment under the Crown,” returned the gray-haired Marquis. “Still, there are serious difficulties in the way.”
And these were the only difficulties which the Marquis saw at the outset of his son’s career.
“My son, the Comte d’Esgrignon, cannot make his appearance at court like a tatterdemalion,” he continued after a pause, marked by a sigh; “he must be equipped. Alas! for these two hundred years we have had no retainers. Ah! Chevalier, this demolition from top to bottom always brings me back to the first hammer stroke delivered by M. de Mirabeau. The one thing needful nowadays is money; that is all that the Revolution has done that I can see. The King does not ask you whether you are a descendant of the Valois or a conquerer of Gaul; he asks whether you pay a thousand francs in tailles which nobles never used to pay. So I cannot well send the Count to court without a matter of twenty thousand crowns﻿—”
“Yes,” assented the Chevalier, “with that trifling sum he could cut a brave figure.”
“Well,” said Mlle. Armande, “I have asked Chesnel to come tonight. Would you believe it, Chevalier, ever since the day when Chesnel proposed that I should marry that miserable du Croisier﻿—”
“Ah! that was truly unworthy, mademoiselle!” cried the Chevalier.
“Unpardonable!” said the Marquis.
“Well, since then my brother has never brought himself to ask anything whatsoever of Chesnel,” continued Mlle. Armande.
“Of your old household servant? Why, Marquis, you would do Chesnel honor﻿—an honor which he would gratefully remember till his latest breath.”
“No,” said the Marquis, “the thing is beneath one’s dignity, it seems to me.”
“There is not much question of dignity; it is a matter of necessity,” said the Chevalier, with the trace of a shrug.
“Never,” said the Marquis, riposting with a gesture which decided the Chevalier to risk a great stroke to open his old friend’s eyes.

===Chinese===

“他不应当把才干埋葬在这种小城的坑洞里。他在这里能指望的最好的前程，就是碰上一个诺曼底姑娘”（他学着她家乡的口音），“乡下出身，傻头傻脑，又有钱。他拿她怎么办呢？——娶她做妻子？哦！老天爷！”
“我衷心希望他把婚事推迟，等他在王家谋得某个显要的官职或任命之后再办。”白发苍苍的侯爵答道，“不过，眼下还有严重的困难。”
而这，便是侯爵在儿子前程的起点上看到的全部困难。
“我的儿子德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵，总不能像个叫花子似的在宫廷里露面，”他叹了一口气，停了停，接着说，“他得置办行头。唉！这两百年来，我们连一个跟班都没有了。啊！骑士先生，这场从上到下的大拆毁，总把我带回到德·米拉波先生抡出的第一锤。如今唯一要紧的东西就是钱；在我看来，革命的全部作为，也就这么一点。国王不问你是不是瓦卢瓦的后裔、高卢的征服者；他只问你缴不缴那1000法郎的泰尔税——那种贵族从前从来不服的人口税。所以，不带上个两万科朗，我实在没法送伯爵进宫廷——”
“是啊，”骑士先生附和道，“有了这笔区区之数，他就能撑起一个漂亮的场面。”
“那么，”阿尔芒德小姐说，“我已经请谢内尔今晚过来。骑士先生，您说怪不怪，从那天谢内尔提议让我嫁给那个卑鄙的杜·克鲁瓦西耶——”
“啊！那实在不成体统，小姐！”骑士先生叫道。
“不可饶恕！”侯爵说。
“从那以后，我哥哥就再也不肯向谢内尔开口求任何事情了。”阿尔芒德小姐接着说。
“求您家的老仆人？嗳，侯爵，这可是给谢内尔脸面啊——这份脸面，他会感激涕零地记到咽气。”
“不，”侯爵说，“依我看，这种事有失身份。”
“谈不上什么身份不身份；这是需要。”骑士先生说，微微耸了耸肩。
“绝不。”侯爵还击道，一挥手势——正是这个手势，促使骑士先生下定决心，冒个大险，来打开老朋友的眼界。

===Original===

“Very well,” he said, “since you do not know it, I will tell you myself that Chesnel has let your son have something already, something like﻿—”
“My son is incapable of accepting anything whatever from Chesnel,” the Marquis broke in, drawing himself up as he spoke. “He might have come to *you* to ask you for twenty-five louis﻿—”
“Something like a hundred thousand livres,” said the Chevalier, finishing his sentence.
“The Comte d’Esgrignon owes a hundred thousand livres to a Chesnel!” cried the Marquis, with every sign of deep pain. “Oh! if he were not an only son, he should set out tonight for Mexico with a captain’s commission. A man may be in debt to moneylenders, they charge a heavy interest, and you are quits; that is right enough; but *Chesnel*! a man to whom one is attached!﻿—”
“Yes, our adorable Victurnien has run through a hundred thousand livres, dear Marquis,” resumed the Chevalier, flicking a trace of snuff from his waistcoat; “it is not much, I know. I myself at his age﻿—But, after all, let us let old memories be, Marquis. The Count is living in the provinces; all things taken into consideration, it is not so much amiss. He will not go far; these irregularities are common in men who do great things afterwards﻿—”
“And he is sleeping upstairs, without a word of this to his father,” exclaimed the Marquis.
“Sleeping innocently as a child who has merely got five or six little bourgeoises into trouble, and now must have duchesses,” returned the Chevalier.
“Why, he deserves a lettre de cachet!”
“ ‘They’ have done away with lettres de cachet,” said the Chevalier. “You know what a hubbub there was when they tried to institute a law for special cases. We could not keep the provost’s courts, which M. *de* Bonaparte used to call *commissions militaires*.”
“Well, well; what are we to do if our boys are wild, or turn out scapegraces? Is there no locking them up in these days?” asked the Marquis.
The Chevalier looked at the heartbroken father and lacked courage to answer, “We shall be obliged to bring them up properly.”
“And you have never said a word of this to me, Mlle. d’Esgrignon,” added the Marquis, turning suddenly round upon Mlle. Armande. He never addressed her as Mlle. d’Esgrignon except when he was vexed; usually she was called “my sister.”

===Chinese===

“那好，”他说，“既然您不知道，我就亲自告诉您吧：谢内尔已经给您儿子钱花了，数目大概有——”
“我儿子绝不会接受谢内尔的任何东西。”侯爵打断了他，一边说一边挺直了身子。“他顶多是找您要25个路易——”
“大概有10万里弗尔。”骑士先生替他把话说完。
“德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵欠了一个谢内尔10万里弗尔！”侯爵喊了起来，脸上带着深深的痛楚。“哦！他若不是独子，今夜就该打发他去墨西哥，当一名见习军官。欠放债人的钱还说得过去，他们收重利，两讫了事；这没有什么不对；可是谢内尔！一个我们如此亲近的人！——”
“是啊，我们可爱的维克图尼安花掉了10万里弗尔，亲爱的侯爵，”骑士先生接着说，弹掉背心上的一点鼻烟屑，“我知道，这不算多。我在他这个年纪——不过，算了，让旧日的记忆去吧，侯爵。伯爵住在外省；通盘考虑起来，这还不算太出格。他走不远；日后成大器的人，年轻时谁没有些荒唐事——”
“而他却睡在楼上，对他父亲只字不提。”侯爵嚷道。
“睡得像孩子一样天真，一个不过让五六个小市民姑娘惹了麻烦、如今该换公爵夫人的孩子。”骑士先生回敬道。
“嗬，他该进密札！”
“‘他们’已经把密札废掉了，”骑士先生说，“当年他们想设立一项专案法庭的法令时，你该记得闹了多大一场乱子。连总督法庭我们都保不住——德·波拿巴先生管它们叫军事委员会。”
“唉，唉；要是我们的孩子野性难驯，或者成了浪子，那可怎么办？如今就没有关他们的法子了？”侯爵问。
骑士先生望着这位心碎的父亲，没有勇气回答：“那我们就只好好好教养他们了。”
“这件事您也从来没有对我提过一个字，德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐。”侯爵补上一句，突然转身对着阿尔芒德小姐。只有心里恼火的时候，他才会称呼她“德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐”；平日里，他都叫她“我的妹妹”。

===Original===

“Why, monsieur, when a young man is full of life and spirits, and leads an idle life in a town like this, what else can you expect?” asked Mlle. d’Esgrignon. She could not understand her brother’s anger.
“Debts! eh! why, hang it all!” added the Chevalier. “He plays cards, he has little adventures, he shoots﻿—all these things are horribly expensive nowadays.”
“Come,” said the Marquis, “it is time to send him to the King. I will spend tomorrow morning in writing to our kinsmen.”
“I have some acquaintance with the Ducs de Navarreins, de Lenoncourt, de Maufrigneuse, and de Chaulieu,” said the Chevalier, though he knew, as he spoke, that he was pretty thoroughly forgotten.
“My dear Chevalier, there is no need of such formalities to present a d’Esgrignon at court,” the Marquis broke in.﻿—“A hundred thousand livres,” he muttered; “this Chesnel makes very free. This is what comes of these accursed troubles. M. Chesnel protects my son. And now I must ask him.﻿ ﻿… No, sister, you must undertake this business. Chesnel shall secure himself for the whole amount by a mortgage on our lands. And just give this harebrained boy a good scolding; he will end by ruining himself if he goes on like this.”
The Chevalier and Mlle. d’Esgrignon thought these words perfectly simple and natural, absurd as they would have sounded to any other listener. So far from seeing anything ridiculous in the speech, they were both very much touched by a look of something like anguish in the old noble’s face. Some dark premonition seemed to weigh upon M. d’Esgrignon at that moment, some glimmering of an insight into the changed times. He went to the settee by the fireside and sat down, forgetting that Chesnel would be there before long; that Chesnel, of whom he could not bring himself to ask anything.
Just then the Marquis d’Esgrignon looked exactly as any imagination with a touch of romance could wish. He was almost bald, but a fringe of silken, white locks, curled at the tips, covered the back of his head.

===Chinese===

“哎，先生，一个年轻人血气方刚，又在这种小城里闲着无事，您还能指望什么呢？”德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐问。她无法理解哥哥的怒气。
“债务！嗐！有什么了不得的！”骑士先生添了一句，“他打牌，他搞点风流事，他打猎——这些事如今都是贵得吓人的。”
“这样吧，”侯爵说，“该送他到国王那里去了。明天一个上午，我要用来给我们的亲友写信。”
“我同德·纳夫兰公爵、德·勒农库公爵、德·莫弗里涅斯公爵和德·肖利厄公爵都有些交情。”骑士先生说，尽管他一边说一边心里明白，人家早把他忘得干干净净了。
“亲爱的骑士，要引荐一位埃斯格里尼翁进宫廷，用不着这些客套。”侯爵打断了他。——“10万里弗尔，”他喃喃地说，“这个谢内尔真是自作主张。这都是那些该死的动荡惹出来的。谢内尔先生保护了我的儿子。如今倒要我开口求他了。……不，妹妹，这件事得由您来办。让谢内尔拿我们的土地作抵押，把这笔钱保全起来。还有，好好训一顿这个糊涂小子；他再这样下去，迟早要把自己毁掉。”
骑士先生和德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐觉得这些话再简单、再自然不过，尽管在别的任何人听来都会觉得荒唐。他们非但不觉得这番话有什么可笑，反倒被老贵族脸上那种近乎痛苦的神情深深打动了。那一刻，某种阴暗的预感似乎压在德·埃斯格里尼翁先生心头，某种对时移世易的隐隐的领悟。他走到壁炉边的长椅上坐下，忘了谢内尔不久就要到来；忘了那个他怎么也开不了口去求的谢内尔。
这时候的德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵，恰是任何带几分浪漫色彩的想象力所期望的模样。他几乎已经秃顶，但后脑还覆着一圈丝一般的白发，发尖微卷。

===Original===

All the pride of race might be seen in a noble forehead, such as you may admire in a Louis XV, a Beaumarchais, a Maréchal de Richelieu; it was not the square, broad brow of the portraits of the Maréchal de Saxe; nor yet the small hard circle of Voltaire, compact to overfulness; it was graciously rounded and finely moulded, the temples were ivory tinted and soft; and mettle and spirit, unquenched by age, flashed from the brilliant eyes. The Marquis had the Condé nose and the lovable Bourbon mouth, from which, as they used to say of the Comte d’Artois, only witty and urbane words proceed. His cheeks, sloping rather than foolishly rounded to the chin, were in keeping with his spare frame, thin legs, and plump hands. The strangulation cravat at his throat was of the kind which every marquis wears in all the portraits which adorn eighteenth century literature; it is common alike to Saint-Preux and to Lovelace, to the elegant Montesquieu’s heroes and to Diderot’s homespun characters (see the first editions of those writers’ works).
The Marquis always wore a white, gold-embroidered, high waistcoat, with the red ribbon of a commander of the Order of St. Louis blazing upon his breast; and a blue coat with wide skirts, and fleur-de-lys on the flaps, which were turned back﻿—an odd costume which the King had adopted. But the Marquis could not bring himself to give up the Frenchman’s knee-breeches nor yet the white silk stockings or the buckles at the knees. After six o’clock in the evening he appeared in full dress.
He read no newspapers but the *Quotidienne* and the *Gazette de France*, two journals accused by the Constitutional press of obscurantist views and uncounted “monarchical and religious” enormities; while the Marquis d’Esgrignon, on the other hand, found heresies and revolutionary doctrines in every issue. No matter to what extremes the organs of this or that opinion may go, they will never go quite far enough to please the purists on their own side; even as the portrayer of this magnificent personage is pretty certain to be accused of exaggeration, whereas he has done his best to soften down some of the cruder tones and dim the more startling tints of the original.
The Marquis d’Esgrignon rested his elbows on his knees and leant his head on his hands. During his meditations Mlle.

===Chinese===

种族的全部骄傲，都写在那高贵的额头上——那种额头，你在路易十五、博马舍、黎塞留元帅的画像上可以欣赏到；它不是萨克森元帅画像上那种方正宽阔的额头；也不是伏尔泰那小小的、硬实得过分的圆额头；它圆润优雅，造型精美，太阳穴泛着象牙般的柔光；未被岁月磨灭的英气与精神，在明亮的眼睛里闪动。侯爵有孔代家的鼻子和波旁家招人喜爱的嘴——正如人们从前说起阿图瓦伯爵的那样，从这张嘴里吐出来的只有机智而文雅的话。他的双颊不朝下巴愚蠢地滚圆，而是略呈坡势，同他瘦削的身架、细瘦的双腿和丰腴的双手正相配。他颈间勒紧的领巾，是十八世纪文学的一切画像里每位侯爵都戴的那一种；圣普乐和洛夫莱斯有它，优雅的孟德斯鸠笔下的英雄有它，狄德罗笔下的土布人物也有它（见这两位作家著作的初版本）。
侯爵永远穿一件白色绣金的高背心，胸前闪着圣路易骑士团指挥官级的红绶带；一件下摆宽大的蓝外套，翻领上绣着百合花，翻卷着——这是国王自己采用的古怪装束。但侯爵怎么也舍不得放弃法国人的及膝短裤、白丝长袜和膝上的扣环。晚上6点以后，他便一身盛装出现。
他只读《每日新闻》和《法兰西报》——这两家报纸被宪政派报刊指控为蒙昧主义、犯下无数“君主与宗教”的弥天大罪；而德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵呢，却在每一期里都找出异端邪说和革命学说。不管这种或那种意见的喉舌走到怎样的极端，也永远不够极端到让自家的纯粹派满意；正如这位堂堂人物的描画者几乎准会被人指控夸张，其实他已尽力冲淡原件的若干粗粝色调、遮暗若干惊人的颜色了。德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵把手肘撑在膝上，头伏在手里。他沉思的当口，阿尔芒德小姐

===Original===

Armande and the Chevalier looked at one another without uttering the thoughts in their minds. Was he pained by the discovery that his son’s future must depend upon his sometime land steward? Was he doubtful of the reception awaiting the young Count? Did he regret that he had made no preparation for launching his heir into that brilliant world of court? Poverty had kept him in the depths of his province; how should he have appeared at court? He sighed heavily as he raised his head.
That sigh, in those days, came from the real aristocracy all over France; from the loyal provincial noblesse, consigned to neglect with most of those who had drawn sword and braved the storm for the cause.
“What have the Princes done for the du Guénics, or the Fontaines, or the Bauvans, who never submitted?” he muttered to himself. “They fling miserable pensions to the men who fought most bravely, and give them a royal lieutenancy in a fortress somewhere on the outskirts of the kingdom.”
Evidently the Marquis doubted the reigning dynasty. Mlle. d’Esgrignon was trying to reassure her brother as to the prospects of the journey, when a step outside on the dry narrow footway gave them notice of Chesnel’s coming. In another moment Chesnel appeared; Joséphin, the Count’s gray-aired valet, admitted the notary without announcing him.
“Chesnel, my boy﻿—” (Chesnel was a white-haired man of sixty-nine, with a square-jawed, venerable countenance; he wore knee-breeches, ample enough to fill several chapters of dissertation in the manner of Sterne, ribbed stockings, shoes with silver clasps, an ecclesiastical-looking coat and a high waistcoat of scholastic cut.)
“Chesnel, my boy, it was very presumptuous of you to lend money to the Comte d’Esgrignon! If I repaid you at once and we never saw each other again, it would be no more than you deserve for giving wings to his vices.”
There was a pause, a silence such as there falls at court when the King publicly reprimands a courtier. The old notary looked humble and contrite.
“I am anxious about that boy, Chesnel,” continued the Marquis in a kindly tone; “I should like to send him to Paris to serve His Majesty. Make arrangements with my sister for his suitable appearance at court.﻿—And we will settle accounts﻿—”
The Marquis looked grave as he left the room with a friendly gesture of farewell to Chesnel.
“I thank M.

===Chinese===

和骑士先生面面相觑，各自把心里想的话咽了回去。儿子的前程竟要仰仗昔日的土地管家，这发现刺痛了他吗？他是在担心年轻伯爵将受到怎样的接待吗？他是在懊悔不曾为把继承人送进那个辉煌的宫廷世界作准备吗？贫穷把他困在外省的深处；不然他怎么在宫廷里露面呢？他抬起头，重重地叹了一口气。
在那个年代，这声叹息出自全法兰西真正的贵族；出自忠于王室的外省贵族——他们同大多数曾为那个事业拔剑迎着风暴而上的人一起，被打入了冷宫。
“亲王们为从未屈服过的德·盖尼克家、德·丰坦家、德·博旺家做过什么呢？”他自言自语地咕哝，“他们把可怜的养老金扔给作战最勇敢的人，赏一个什么王国边陲要塞的王家中尉完事。”
显然，侯爵对当朝的王朝心存怀疑。德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐正想就这趟旅程的前景宽慰哥哥，外面干燥的人行窄道上传来脚步声，通报谢内尔来了。转眼间谢内尔走了进来；伯爵那位灰白头发的贴身老仆若瑟兰，没等通报就把公证人让了进来。
“谢内尔，我的老伙计——”（谢内尔是个69岁的白发老者，方面阔颐，相貌庄重；他穿及膝短裤——那裤子的褶子够斯特恩式的文人体例写上好几章论文——棱纹长袜，银扣鞋，一件教士模样的外衣和一件学究式剪裁的高背心。）
“谢内尔，我的老伙计，你借钱给德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵，实在是太胆大妄为了！假使我立刻把钱还清，从此彼此不再见面，那也不过是你的应得——谁叫你给他的恶习插上翅膀。”
接着是一阵停顿，一阵沉默，就像宫廷里国王当众申斥朝臣时落下的那种沉默。老公证人的样子谦卑而惶恐。
“我是为这孩子担心呐，谢内尔，”侯爵换上和气的口吻接着说，“我想送他上巴黎，为陛下效力。请同我妹妹商量，把他在宫廷里露面的事安排妥当。——账，我们也会结清的——”
侯爵神色庄重地离开了客厅，友好地向谢内尔挥手作别。
“我感谢侯爵

===Original===

le Marquis for all his goodness,” returned the old man, who still remained standing.
Mlle. Armande rose to go to the door with her brother; she had rung the bell, old Joséphin was in readiness to light his master to his room.
“Take a seat, Chesnel,” said the lady, as she returned, and with womanly tact she explained away and softened the Marquis’ harshness. And yet beneath that harshness Chesnel saw a great affection. The Marquis’ attachment for his old servant was something of the same order as a man’s affection for his dog; he will fight anyone who kicks the animal, the dog is like a part of his existence, a something which, if not exactly himself, represents him in that which is nearest and dearest﻿—his sensibilities.
“It is quite time that M. le Comte should be sent away from the town, mademoiselle,” he said sententiously.
“Yes,” returned she. “Has he been indulging in some new escapade?”
“No, mademoiselle.”
“Well, why do you blame him?”
“I am not blaming him, mademoiselle. No, I am not blaming him. I am very far from blaming him. I will even say that I shall never blame him, whatever he may do.”
There was a pause. The Chevalier, nothing if not quick to take in a situation, began to yawn like a sleep-ridden mortal. Gracefully he made his excuses and went, with as little mind to sleep as to go and drown himself. The imp Curiosity kept the Chevalier wide awake, and with airy fingers plucked away the cotton wool from his ears.
“Well, Chesnel, is it something new?” Mlle. Armande began anxiously.
“Yes, things that cannot be told to M. le Marquis; he would drop down in an apoplectic fit.”
“Speak out,” she said. With her beautiful head leant on the back of her low chair, and her arms extended listlessly by her side, she looked as if she were waiting passively for her deathblow.
“Mademoiselle, M. le Comte, with all his cleverness, is a plaything in the hands of mean creatures, petty natures on the lookout for a crushing revenge. They want to ruin us and bring us low! There is the President of the Tribunal, M. de Ronceret; he has, as you know, a very great notion of his descent﻿—”
“His grandfather was an attorney,” interposed Mlle. Armande.
“I know he was. And for that reason you have not received him; nor does he go to M. de Troisville’s, nor to M. le Duc de Verneuil’s, nor to the Marquis de Castéran’s; but he is one of the pillars of du Croisier’s salon.

先生的一片好意，”老人答道，仍旧站在原地。
阿尔芒德小姐起身送哥哥到门口；她已拉过铃，老若瑟兰正候着，准备掌灯送主人回房。
“请坐，谢内尔。”女士回身说道；她以女性的圆通，把侯爵的严厉解释过去、软化下来。然而在那严厉底下，谢内尔看出一片深情。侯爵对老仆人的依恋，同一个人对爱犬的感情属于同一种：谁踢了那畜生，他会跟谁拼命；那狗仿佛是他生命的一部分，某种东西——虽不就是他自己，却在最贴近他、最亲爱的东西即他的情感方面代表着他自己。
“小姐，该把伯爵先生送出这座城了。”他一字一板地说。
“是啊，”她答道，“他又干什么荒唐事了？”
“没有，小姐。”
“那您为什么责备他？”
“我不是在责备他，小姐。不，我不是在责备他。我离责备他还远着呢。我甚至可以说，无论他做出什么事来，我也永远不会责备他。”
一阵沉默。骑士先生最善于体察情势，随即像困得要命的人一样打起呵欠来。他优雅地道了歉，告辞而去，其实心里既不想睡觉，也不想去投河。好奇这个小鬼让骑士先生毫无睡意，用轻巧的手指把他耳朵里的棉花统统掏了出来。
“怎么样，谢内尔，出了什么新鲜事吗？”阿尔芒德小姐焦急地开了口。
“是的，是些没法对侯爵先生讲的事；一听他会当场中风倒下。”
“说出来吧。”她说。她那美丽的头仰在矮椅背上，两臂无精打采地垂在身侧，那样子仿佛在消极地等着致命的一击。
“小姐，伯爵先生那么聪明，却成了一伙卑鄙小人的掌中玩物，那些心胸狭隘的家伙正伺机进行毁灭性的报复。他们要毁掉我们，把我们踩到地上！法院院长迪·龙瑟雷就是其中的一个；您知道，他把自己的家世看得可了不得——”
“他祖父当过讼师。”阿尔芒德小姐插了一句。
“我知道。正因为这个，您没有接待过他；他也不上德·特鲁瓦维尔先生家，不上德·韦尔讷伊公爵家，不上德·卡斯特朗侯爵家；可他是杜·克鲁瓦西耶客厅的顶梁柱之一。

===Original===

Your nephew may rub shoulders with young M. Fabien du Ronceret without condescending too far, for he must have companions of his own age. Well and good. That young fellow is at the bottom of all M. le Comte’s follies; he and two or three of the rest of them belong to the other side, the side of M. le Chevalier’s enemy, who does nothing but breathe threats of vengeance against you and all the nobles together. They all hope to ruin you through your nephew. The ringleader of the conspiracy is this sycophant of a du Croisier, the pretended Royalist. Du Croisier’s wife, poor thing, knows nothing about it; you know her, I should have heard of it before this if she had ears to hear evil. For some time these wild young fellows were not in the secret, nor was anybody else; but the ringleaders let something drop in jest, and then the fools got to know about it, and after the Count’s recent escapades they let fall some words while they were drunk. And those words were carried to me by others who are sorry to see such a fine, handsome, noble, charming lad ruining himself with pleasure. So far people feel sorry for him; before many days are over they will﻿—I am afraid to say what﻿—”
“They will despise him; say it out, Chesnel!” Mlle. Armande cried piteously.
“Ah! How can you keep the best people in the town from finding out faults in their neighbors? They do not know what to do with themselves from morning to night. And so M. le Comte’s losses at play are all reckoned up. Thirty thousand francs have taken flight during these two months, and everybody wonders where he gets the money. If they mention it when I am present, I just call them to order. Ah! but﻿—‘Do you suppose’ (I told them this morning), ‘do you suppose that if the d’Esgrignon family have lost their manorial rights, that therefore they have been robbed of their hoard of treasure? The young Count has a right to do as he pleases; and so long as he does not owe you a halfpenny, you have no right to say a word.’ ”
Mlle. Armande held out her hand, and the notary kissed it respectfully.
“Good Chesnel!﻿ ﻿… But, my friend, how shall we find the money for this journey? Victurnien must appear as befits his rank at court.”
“Oh! I have borrowed money on Le Jard, mademoiselle.”
“What? You have nothing left! Ah, heaven! what can we do to reward you?”
“You can take the hundred thousand francs which I hold at your disposal.

===Chinese===

您侄儿同小法比安·迪·龙瑟雷厮混，倒算不上太屈尊，因为他总得有同龄的伙伴。这且不坏。可是那小子是伯爵先生一切荒唐事的根子；他和另外两三个同类是另一边的人，是骑士先生那位仇敌一边的——那人一天到晚把对你们和全体贵族的报复挂在嘴上。他们全都指望通过您侄儿毁掉你们。这场阴谋的头子，就是那个马屁精杜·克鲁瓦西耶，那个假保王党。杜·克鲁瓦西耶的妻子，可怜的女人，对此一无所知；您认识她，她要是听得进一句坏话，我早该有所耳闻了。起初，这些浪荡青年并不知情，别人也不知情；可主谋们说笑间漏了点口风，那些蠢货便知道了底细；伯爵最近闯了祸之后，他们喝醉时又漏出过几句。这些话传到我耳朵里，是靠了另外几个人——他们眼看着这样一个俊美、高贵、可爱的青年恣意毁掉自己，心里不忍。眼下人们还只是替他惋惜；过不了几天，他们就要——我怕说出那个字来——”
“他们要看不起他了；说出来吧，谢内尔！”阿尔芒德小姐凄楚地喊道。
“唉！您有什么法子拦住城里最好的人不去挑邻居的错处？他们从早到晚闲得没事干。于是伯爵先生牌桌上的输账都给算了出来。两个月里飞掉了3万法郎，人人都纳闷他的钱是哪里来的。他们当着我的面提起这事，我就把他们喝止住。啊！不过——‘你们以为’（今天早晨我对他们说了），‘你们以为德·埃斯格里尼翁家丢了领主权利，就等于把家底的金山也丢了？年轻伯爵爱怎么花就怎么花，这是他的权利；只要他不久你们一个铜子，你们就无权说三道四。’”
阿尔芒德小姐伸出手来，公证人恭敬地吻了吻。
“好谢内尔！……可是，我的朋友，这趟出行，我们上哪儿筹钱去？维克图尼安在宫廷里露面，总得配得上他的身份。”
“哦！我拿莱雅尔庄园抵押借了钱，小姐。”
“什么？您什么都没剩了！啊，天哪！我们拿什么报答您呢？”
“您尽管收下这10万法郎，我随时听候您支取。

===Original===

You can understand that the loan was negotiated in confidence, so that it might not reflect on you; for it is known in the town that I am closely connected with the d’Esgrignon family.”
Tears came into Mlle. Armande’s eyes. Chesnel saw them, took a fold of the noble woman’s dress in his hands, and kissed it.
“Never mind,” he said, “a lad must sow his wild oats. In great salons in Paris his boyish ideas will take a new turn. And, really, though our old friends here are the worthiest folk in the world, and no one could have nobler hearts than they, they are not amusing. If M. le Comte wants amusement, he is obliged to look below his rank, and he will end by getting into low company.”
Next day the old traveling coach saw the light, and was sent to be put in repair. In a solemn interview after breakfast, the hope of the house was duly informed of his father’s intentions regarding him﻿—he was to go to court and ask to serve His Majesty. He would have time during the journey to make up his mind about his career. The navy or the army, the privy council, an embassy, or the Royal Household﻿—all were open to a d’Esgrignon, a d’Esgrignon had only to choose. The King would certainly look favorably upon the d’Esgrignons, because they had asked nothing of him, and had sent the youngest representative of their house to receive the recognition of Majesty.
But young d’Esgrignon, with all his wild pranks, had guessed instinctively what society in Paris meant, and formed his own opinions of life. So when they talked of his leaving the country and the paternal roof, he listened with a grave countenance to his revered parent’s lecture, and refrained from giving him a good deal of information in reply. As, for instance, that young men no longer went into the army or the navy as they used to do; that if a man had a mind to be a second lieutenant in a cavalry regiment without passing through a special training in the Écoles, he must first serve in the Pages; that sons of the greatest houses went exactly like commoners to Saint-Cyr and the École polytechnique, and took their chances of being beaten by base blood.

===Chinese===

这笔借款是悄悄办妥的，您自然明白，免得牵连你们的名声；城里人都知道我同德·埃斯格里尼翁家关系密切。”
泪水涌上了阿尔芒德小姐的眼睛。谢内尔看见了，双手捧起这位贵妇衣裙的一角，吻了吻。
“不要紧，”他说，“年轻人总要放荡放荡。在巴黎的大客厅里，他那孩子气的念头会转个方向的。说真的，我们这里的老朋友虽然都是世上最可敬的人，心肠再高贵不过，可他们实在无趣。伯爵先生要想寻欢作乐，只好到身份比他低的人堆里去找，到头来便会混进下流的圈子。”
第二天，那辆旧旅行马车重见天日，送去修理了。早饭以后，一场庄严的谈话中，全家的希望正式获悉了父亲对他的打算——他要进宫廷，请求为陛下效力。旅途上他会有时间拿定主意选定前程。海军也罢，陆军也罢，枢密院、使馆或王室内府也罢——对埃斯格里尼翁家的人样样敞开着，埃斯格里尼翁家的人只需挑一挑。国王肯定会眷顾德·埃斯格里尼翁家，因为他们从没有向他求过什么，如今又把家族最年轻的代表送来领受御颜的眷顾。
可是小德·埃斯格里尼翁尽管荒唐，却凭本能猜到了巴黎社交界的底细，对人生自有一套见解。所以当人们谈起他要离开乡土和父宅时，他神情庄重地聆听可敬的父亲的训示，却克制着不拿许多实情回禀。比如：年轻人不再像从前那样进陆军或海军了；谁要想不经专门学校的特别训练就当上骑兵部队的少尉，就必须先在御前侍童里当差；最显赫人家的儿子同平民子弟一样进圣西尔军校和综合理工学院，甘冒被寒门子弟压倒的风险。

===Original===

If he had enlightened his relatives on these points, funds might not have been forthcoming for a stay in Paris; so he allowed his father and Aunt Armande to believe that he would be permitted a seat in the King’s carriages, that he must support his dignity at court as the d’Esgrignon of the time, and rub shoulders with great lords of the realm.
It grieved the Marquis that he could send but one servant with his son; but he gave him his own valet Joséphin, a man who can be trusted to take care of his young master, and to watch faithfully over his interests. The poor father must do without Joséphin, and hope to replace him with a young lad.
“Remember that you are a Carol, my boy,” he said; “remember that you come of an unalloyed descent, and that your scutcheon bears the motto *Cil est nostre*; with such arms you may hold your head high everywhere, and aspire to queens. Render grace to your father, as I to mine. We owe it to the honor of our ancestors, kept stainless until now, that we can look all men in the face, and need bend the knee to none save a mistress, the King, and God. This is the greatest of your privileges.”
Chesnel, good man, was breakfasting with the family. He took no part in counsels based on heraldry, nor in the inditing of letters addressed to divers mighty personages of the day; but he had spent the night in writing to an old friend of his, one of the oldest established notaries of Paris. Without this letter it is not possible to understand Chesnel’s real and assumed fatherhood. It almost recalls Daedalus’ address to Icarus; for where, save in old mythology, can you look for comparisons worthy of this man of antique mould?

===Chinese===

倘若他在这些点上点破了亲人们，巴黎之行的经费也许就下不来了；于是他索性让父亲和阿尔芒德姑妈相信，他会获准在国王的御辇里有一席之地，他必须以当今埃斯格里尼翁的身份在宫廷里撑住门面，同王国的显贵们平起平坐。
侯爵很痛心，只能派一个仆人随儿子去；他把自己的贴身老仆若瑟兰给了他，这个人靠得住，会照料年轻的主人，忠心看守他的利益。可怜的父亲只好自己将就着没有若瑟兰伺候，指望再找个年轻小子补上。
“记住你是一个卡罗尔，我的孩子，”他说，“记住你出身于纯正无杂的血统，你的纹章上镌着铭言‘Cil est nostre’（此即吾属）；凭着这样的纹章，你在哪里都可以昂首挺胸，连王后也可以存几分指望。要感念你的父亲，如同我感念我的父亲。多亏我们先祖的名誉至今清白无瑕，我们才敢正视一切人，除了情人、国王和上帝，不必向任何人下跪。这是你所享有的最大特权。”
好人谢内尔正同主人家一起用早餐。他不参与那些以纹章学为依据的谋划，也不参与给当朝各位大人物写信的文墨事；可他花了一整夜，给一位老朋友——巴黎开业最早的公证人之一——写信。不读这封信，便无从理解谢内尔那名副其实又自居名分的父爱。它几乎令人想起代达罗斯对伊卡洛斯的叮嘱；因为除了古老的神话，你还能到哪里去为这位古风犹存的人物找到相称的比拟呢？

===Original===

> “My dear and estimable Sorbier﻿—I remember with no little pleasure that I made my first campaign in our honorable profession under your father, and that you had a liking for me, poor little clerk that I was. And now I appeal to old memories of the days when we worked in the same office, old pleasant memories for our hearts, to ask you to do me the one service that I have ever asked of you in the course of our long lives, crossed as they have been by political catastrophes, to which, perhaps, I owe it that I have the honor to be your colleague. And now I ask this service of you, my friend, and my white hairs will be brought with sorrow to the grave if you should refuse my entreaty. It is no question of myself or of mine, Sorbier, for I lost poor Mme. Chesnel, and I have no child of my own. Something more to me than my own family (if I had one) is involved﻿—it is the Marquis d’Esgrignon’s only son. I have had the honor to be the Marquis’ land steward ever since I left the office to which his father sent me at his own expense, with the idea of providing for me. The house which nurtured me has passed through all the troubles of the Revolution. I have managed to save some of their property; but what is it, after all, in comparison with the wealth that they have lost? I cannot tell you, Sorbier, how deeply I am attached to the great house, which has been all but swallowed up under my eyes by the abyss of time. M. le Marquis was proscribed, and his lands confiscated, he was getting on in years, he had no child. Misfortunes upon misfortunes! Then M. le Marquis married, and his wife died when the young Count was born, and today this noble, dear, and precious child is all the life of the d’Esgrignon family; the fate of the house hangs upon him. He has got into debt here with amusing himself. What else should he do in the provinces with an allowance of a miserable hundred louis? Yes, my friend, a hundred louis, the great house has come to this.
> “In this extremity his father thinks it necessary to send the Count to Paris to ask for the King’s favor at court. Paris is a very dangerous place for a lad; if he is to keep steady there, he must have the grain of sense which makes notaries of us. Besides, I should be heartbroken to think of the poor boy living amid such hardships as we have known.

===Chinese===

> “我亲爱的、可敬的索尔比耶——我怀着不小的快慰记得，我在我们这一体面行当里的头一趟差事，是在令尊手下当差，而您对我这个可怜的小文书也颇有好感。如今我求助于我们同在一间办公室里共事的岁月的那些旧日记忆——那些留存在我们心中的美好记忆——来求您为我办一件事；在我们漫长的一生中，被种种政治变故横加阻隔的一生中，我还从未求过您什么——也许正亏了那些变故，我才有幸与您结为同行。如今我向您恳求这件事，我的朋友；倘若您拒绝我的恳求，我的白发将含悲带恨地进入坟墓。这完全不是为了我自己或我自己的家人，索尔比耶，因为我失去了可怜的谢内尔太太，又没有亲生儿女。牵涉在内的，是比我家亲人（倘若我有）更亲的——是德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵的独子。自从侯爵的父亲自掏腰包送我进公证人事务所、为我筹谋前程以来，我始终有幸担任侯爵的田庄管家。养育我的这个门第，经历了革命的全部风浪。我设法为他们保住了几处产业；可是同他们失去的财富相比，那又算得了什么？我无法向您言传，索尔比耶，我对这个名门眷恋有多深——它险些在我眼前被时代的深渊整个吞没。侯爵先生曾遭放逐，田产被没收，他年事渐高，膝下无儿。灾祸接着灾祸！后来侯爵先生续了弦，年轻伯爵出生时夫人去世了；如今，这个高贵、可爱、珍贵的孩子就是德·埃斯格里尼翁家的全部生命；这个门第的命运系于他一身。他在这里寻欢作乐，欠下了债。在外省，一个月100来个路易的可怜零用，他还能干什么别的呢？是的，我的朋友，100个路易，这个名门就落到了这步田地。
> “在这紧要关头，他父亲认为必须送伯爵去巴黎，到宫廷乞求国王的恩典。巴黎对一个青年是个非常危险的地方；他若要在那里站稳脚跟，就必须有那一点把我们造就成公证人的见识。再说，一想到这可怜的孩子过我们当年那种苦日子，我就心碎。

===Original===

﻿—Do you remember the pleasure with which you shared my roll in the pit of the Théâtre-Français when we spent a day and a night there waiting to see *The Marriage of Figaro*? Oh, blind that we were!﻿—We were happy and poor, but a noble cannot be happy in poverty. A noble in want﻿—it is a thing against nature! Ah! Sorbier, when one has known the satisfaction of propping one of the grandest genealogical trees in the kingdom in its fall, it is so natural to interest oneself in it and to grow fond of it, and love it and water it and look to see it blossom. So you will not be surprised at so many precautions on my part; you will not wonder when I beg the help of your lights, so that all may go well with our young man.
> “The family has allowed a hundred thousand francs for the expenses of M. le Comte’s journey. There is not a young man in Paris fit to compare with him, as you will see! You will take an interest in him as if he were your only son; and lastly, I am quite sure that Madame Sorbier will not hesitate to second you in the office of guardian. M. le Comte Victurnien’s monthly allowance is fixed at two thousand francs, but give him ten thousand for his preliminary expenses. The family have provided in this way for a stay of two years, unless he takes a journey a broad, in which case we will see about making other arrangements. Join me in this work, my old friend, and keep the purse-strings fairly tight. Represent things to M. le Comte without reproving him; hold him in as far as you can, and do not let him anticipate his monthly allowance without sufficient reason, for he must not be driven to desperation if honor is involved.
> “Keep yourself informed of his movements and doings, of the company which he keeps, and watch over his connections with women. M. le Chevalier says that an opera dancer often costs less than a court lady. Obtain information on that point and let me know. If you are too busy, perhaps Mme. Sorbier might know what becomes of the young man, and where he goes. The idea of playing the part of guardian angel to such a noble and charming boy might have attractions for her. God will remember her for accepting the sacred trust. Perhaps when you see M. le Comte Victurnien, her heart may tremble at the thought of all the dangers awaiting him in Paris; he is very young, and very handsome; clever, and at the same time disposed to trust others.

===Chinese===

﻿—你可记得，我们为看《费加罗的婚礼》，在法兰西剧院的正厅后座里守了一天一夜，分吃我那一个面包卷，那份快活？哦，我们那时多盲目！——我们穷得开心；可是贵族不能穷开心。穷贵族——这是违背自然的东西！啊！索尔比耶，一个人一旦领略过扶住王国最显赫的家谱树之一倾而不倒的快慰，自然而然就会关心它、疼爱它，给它浇水，盼着看它开花。所以我这许多未雨绸缪，您就不会奇怪了；我恳请您指点相助，好让我们年轻人一切顺遂，您也不会见怪吧。
> “家里拨了10万法郎作伯爵先生此行的费用。巴黎没有一个青年比得上他，您见了就知道！您要拿他当独生子一样上心；最后，我深信索尔比耶太太绝不会推辞，会同您一起担起监护人的职分。维克图尼安·德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵的月费定为2000法郎，但请先给他1万法郎作安家费。家里按这个数目备下了两年的用度，除非他出国游历，那样我们再另作安排。同我一起操持这件事吧，我的老友，钱袋口要收得紧些。有事向伯爵先生陈明，但不要训斥他；尽量约束他，没有充足的理由，不要让他预支月费，因为倘若牵涉到名誉，千万不能把他逼上绝路。
> “请随时了解他的行止作为、他交往的圈子，并看住他同女人的来往。骑士先生说过，一个歌剧院的舞女往往比一位宫廷贵妇花的钱少。这方面请打听清楚告诉我。您若太忙，也许索尔比耶太太能知道这青年的动静和去处。给这样一位高贵可爱的少年当守护天使，这个念头或许对她颇有吸引力。她若接受这神圣的托付，上帝会记着她的。也许等见到维克图尼安·德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵，想到巴黎等着他的种种危险，她的心会发颤；他太年轻，又太俊美；人聪明，却又轻信。

===Original===

If he forms a connection with some designing woman, Mme. Sorbier could counsel him better than you yourself could do. The old manservant who is with him can tell you many things; sound Joséphin, I have told him to go to you in delicate matters.
> “But why should I say more? We once were clerks together, and a pair of scamps; remember our escapades, and be a little bit young again, my old friend, in your dealings with him. The sixty thousand francs will be remitted to you in the shape of a bill on the Treasury by a gentlemen who is going to Paris,” and so forth.

===Chinese===

他若同哪个工于心计的女人勾搭上，索尔比耶太太劝起他来，比您本人还合适。跟着他的那位老仆能告诉您许多事；向若瑟兰探探口风，我已经嘱咐过他，遇着微妙的事就去找您。
> “可我何必多说呢？我们曾同做文书，是一对淘气鬼；记得我们当年的荒唐事，同他打交道时，我的老友，您也稍稍年轻一回吧。那6万法郎，将由一位即将赴巴黎的先生以国库汇票的形式汇给您。”以下等等。

===Original===

If the old couple to whom this epistle was addressed had followed out Chesnel’s instructions, they would have been compelled to take three private detectives into their pay. And yet there was ample wisdom shown in Chesnel’s choice of a depositary. A banker pays money to anyone accredited to him so long as the money lasts; whereas, Victurnien was obliged, every time that he was in want of money, to make a personal visit to the notary, who was quite sure to use the right of remonstrance.
Victurnien heard that he was to be allowed two thousand francs every month, and thought that he betrayed his joy. He knew nothing of Paris. He fancied that he could keep up princely state on such a sum.
Next day he started on his journey. All the benedictions of the Collection of Antiquities went with him; he was kissed by the dowagers; good wishes were heaped on his head; his old father, his aunt, and Chesnel went with him out of the town, tears filling the eyes of all three. The sudden departure supplied material for conversation for several evenings; and what was more, it stirred the rancorous minds of the salon du Croisier to the depths. The forage-contractor, the president, and others who had vowed to ruin the d’Esgrignons, saw their prey escaping out of their hands. They had based their schemes of revenge on a young man’s follies, and now he was beyond their reach.
The tendency in human nature, which often gives a bigot a rake for a daughter, and makes a frivolous woman the mother of a narrow pietist; that rule of contraries, which, in all probability, is the “resultant” of the law of similarities, drew Victurnien to Paris by a desire to which he must sooner or later have yielded. Brought up as he had been in the old-fashioned provincial house, among the quiet, gentle faces that smiled upon him, among sober servants attached to the family, and surroundings tinged with a general color of age, the boy had only seen friends worthy of respect. All of those about him, with the exception of the Chevalier, had example of venerable age, were elderly men and women, sedate of manner, decorous and sententious of speech. He had been petted by those women in gray gowns and embroidered mittens described by Blondet.

===Chinese===

倘若收信的这对老夫妇真按谢内尔的嘱咐一一照办，他们只好雇上三个密探。不过谢内尔挑选这位代管人，实有大智慧在。银行家只要钱还没耗尽，凭印鉴见票即付；而维克图尼安每回缺钱，都得亲自登门见公证人，公证人则必定要行使规劝的权利。
维克图尼安听说每月可以支配2000法郎，还以为自己掩饰不住喜悦。他对巴黎一无所知。他以为凭这笔钱就能维持亲王般的排场。
第二天他启程了。古物陈列室全体的祝福随他而去；孀居的老夫人们吻了他；美好的祝愿堆在他头上；老父亲、姑妈和谢内尔送他出城，三双眼眶里都噙着泪。这趟突然的动身，给好几晚的谈资供了材料；更要紧的是，它把杜·克鲁瓦西耶客厅里那伙怀恨的心搅了个底朝天。草料承包商、院长，以及其他发誓要毁掉德·埃斯格里尼翁家的人，眼看猎物从手里脱逃了。他们把复仇的计谋全押在一个年轻人的荒唐上，如今他够不着了。
人性中常使伪善者生出一个浪荡女儿、使轻浮妇人养出一个偏执信徒的那种趋向；那种相反相成的规律——十有八九，它是相似律的“合力”——以一个欲望把维克图尼安引向巴黎，那欲望他迟早总要屈服。他从小生长在旧式的外省宅子里，周围是对他微笑的安静温和的面孔，忠于家族的循规蹈矩的仆人，一切浸着老年的总体色调；这孩子见到的全是可以敬重的人。他身边所有的人，除了骑士先生，都以可敬的高龄为榜样，都是上了年纪的男女，举止凝重，言谈得体而好引箴言。疼爱他的，正是布隆代描写过的那些穿灰裙、戴绣花露指手套的妇人。

===Original===

The antiquated splendors of his father’s house were as little calculated as possible to suggest frivolous thoughts; and lastly, he had been educated by a sincerely religious abbé, possessed of all the charm of old age, which has dwelt in two centuries, and brings to the Present its gifts of the dried roses of experience, the faded flowers of the old customs of its youth. Everything should have combined to fashion Victurnien to serious habits; his whole surroundings from childhood bade him continue the glory of a historic name, by taking his life as something noble and great; and yet Victurnien listened to dangerous promptings.
For him, his noble birth was a stepping-stone which raised him above other men. He felt that the idol of Noblesse, before which they burned incense at home, was hollow; he had come to be one of the commonest as well as one of the worst types from a social point of view﻿—a consistent egoist. The aristocratic cult of the Ego simply taught him to follow his own fancies; he had been idolized by those who had the care of him in childhood, and adored by the companions who shared in his boyish escapades, and so he had formed a habit of looking and judging everything as it affected his own pleasure; he took it as a matter of course when good souls saved him from the consequences of his follies, a piece of mistaken kindness which could only lead to his ruin. Victurnien’s early training, noble and pious though it was, had isolated him too much. He was out of the current of the life of the time, for the life of a provincial town is certainly not in the main current of the age; Victurnien’s true destiny lifted him above it. He had learned to think of an action, not as it affected others, nor relatively, but absolutely from his own point of view. Like despots, he made the law to suit the circumstance, a system which works in the lives of prodigal sons the same confusion which fancy brings into art.
Victurnien was quick-sighted, he saw clearly and without illusion, but he acted on impulse, and unwisely. An indefinable flaw of character, often seen in young men, but impossible to explain, led him to will one thing and do another. In spite of an active mind, which showed itself in unexpected ways, the senses had but to assert themselves, and the darkened brain seemed to exist no longer.

===Chinese===

他父亲宅中那种古旧的富丽，最不宜于诱发轻浮的念头；最后，教他读书的是一位虔诚敬神的教士，具备老年的全部魅力——那种活在两个世纪里的高龄，向“现在”献上它的礼物：经验之干玫瑰，以及它青春时代旧风俗的褪色花朵。一切都本该合力把维克图尼安铸成庄重的习性；他自幼的全部环境都嘱托他把生命当作高贵而伟大的事业，去延续一个历史姓氏的荣光；然而维克图尼安听见的却是危险的怂恿。
在他，贵族出身是一块垫脚石，把他垫到众人之上。他感到家里焚香礼拜的那尊“贵族”偶像，内里是空的；从社会的角度看，他成了最平庸、也最恶劣的典型之一——一个彻头彻尾的自我主义者。对自我的贵族式崇拜，只教会他随心所欲；童年照管他的人把他当偶像供着，同他一起闯祸的伙伴把他奉若神明，于是他养成了一个习惯：一切事物，只看它对自己享乐的影响，并据此评判；善良的人们把他从荒唐的后果里搭救出来，他视为理所当然——这种错爱的仁慈，只能把他引向毁灭。维克图尼安早年所受的训育，尽管高贵而虔诚，却把他隔绝得太厉害了。他置身于时代生活的水流之外，因为外省小城的生活肯定不在时代的主航道里；维克图尼安真正的命运又把他凌驾其上。他学会了这样看待一件行为：不看它如何影响别人，不作相对的衡量，只从自己的角度作绝对的判断。他像专制君主一样，让法律迁就情势——这套做法在败家子的生活里制造的混乱，同幻想在艺术里制造的混乱一模一样。
维克图尼安目光锐利，看得清楚，不带幻想，可是行事凭冲动，失于轻率。一种说不清的性格缺陷——年轻人身上常见，却无法解释——使他想的和做的南辕北辙。尽管他头脑灵活，常有意想不到的表现，可感官一旦抬头，昏了头的脑子仿佛就不复存在了。

===Original===

He might have astonished wise men; he was capable of setting fools agape. His desires, like a sudden squall of bad weather, overclouded all the clear and lucid spaces of his brain in a moment; and then, after the dissipations which he could not resist, he sank, utterly exhausted in body, heart, and mind, into a collapsed condition bordering upon imbecility. Such a character will drag a man down into the mire if he is left to himself, or bring him to the highest heights of political power if he has some stern friend to keep him in hand. Neither Chesnel, nor the lad’s father, nor Aunt Armande had fathomed the depths of a nature so nearly akin on many sides to the poetic temperament, yet smitten with a terrible weakness at its core.
---
By the time the old town lay several miles away, Victurnien felt not the slightest regret; he thought no more about the father, who had loved ten generations in his son, nor of the aunt, and her almost insane devotion. He was looking forward to Paris with vehement ill-starred longings; in thought he had lived in that fairyland, it had been the background of his brightest dreams. He imagined that he would be first in Paris, as he had been in the town and the department where his father’s name was potent; but it was vanity, not pride, that filled his soul, and in his dreams his pleasures were to be magnified by all the greatness of Paris. The distance was soon crossed. The traveling coach, like his own thoughts, left the narrow horizon of the province for the vast world of the great city, without a break in the journey. He stayed in the Rue de Richelieu, in a handsome hotel close to the boulevard, and hastened to take possession of Paris as a famished horse rushes into a meadow.
He was not long in finding out the difference between country and town, and was rather surprised than abashed by the change. His mental quickness soon discovered how small an entity he was in the midst of this all-comprehending Babylon; how insane it would be to attempt to stem the torrent of new ideas and new ways. A single incident was enough. He delivered his father’s letter of introduction to the Duc de Lenoncourt, a noble who stood high in favor with the King. He saw the duke in his splendid mansion, among surroundings befitting his rank. Next day he met him again.

===Chinese===

他本可以令智者惊异；他也做得到叫傻子瞠目。他的欲望像一阵突发的坏天气，刹那间把他头脑里一切清明透亮的角落都罩上阴云；随后，在那些抗拒不了的纵乐之后，他身心俱疲，陷入一种近乎痴愚的委顿状态。这样的性格，放任自流会把人拖进泥潭，若有严师益友约束，也能把人送上政治权力的巅峰。谢内尔也好，少年的父亲也好，阿尔芒德姑妈也好，谁也没有探到这天性的底——它在许多方面酷近诗人的气质，核心处却带着可怕的孱弱。
---
等到老城落在身后好几里，维克图尼安没有半点留恋；他不再想那位把十代人的爱都倾注在儿子身上的父亲，也不再想姑妈和她那近乎痴狂的疼爱。他以炽烈而不祥的渴望盼着巴黎；在想象里，他早已住进那片仙境，它一直是他最明亮的梦境的背景。他以为自己在巴黎也将首屈一指，如同在这座城、这个郡——父亲的名字在这里是有威势的；可是充盈他灵魂的是虚荣，而不是自尊；在梦中，他的种种享乐还要借巴黎的全部伟大放大开去。路程很快走完。旅行马车同他的思路一样，中途不作停留，从外省狭窄的地平线驶进了大都会的广阔世界。他下榻在黎塞留街，离林荫大道不远的一家漂亮旅馆，随即扑向巴黎，占有巴黎，像一匹饿马冲进牧场。
他不久就发现了外省与都城的差别，这变化与其说叫他狼狈，不如说叫他吃惊。他脑子快，立刻明白了自己在这包罗万象的巴比伦中间是多么渺小的一个存在；明白想逆着新思想、新风俗的洪流而上是何等疯狂。一件小事就够了。他把父亲的引荐信送呈德·勒农库公爵——一位深得国王欢心的贵族。他在公爵富丽堂皇的宅邸里，在与其身份相称的排场中见到了他。第二天，他又见到了他。

===Original===

This time the Peer of France was lounging on foot along the boulevard, just like any ordinary mortal, with an umbrella in his hand; he did not even wear the Blue Ribbon, without which no knight of the order could have appeared in public in other times. And, duke and peer and first gentleman of the bedchamber though he was, M. de Lenoncourt, in spite of his high courtesy, could not repress a smile as he read his relative’s letter; and that smile told Victurnien that the Collection of Antiquities and the Tuileries were separated by more than sixty leagues of road; the distance of several centuries lay between them.
The names of the families grouped about the throne are quite different in each successive reign, and the characters change with the names. It would seem that, in the sphere of court, the same thing happens over and over again in each generation; but each time there is a quite different set of personages. If history did not prove that this is so, it would seem incredible. The prominent men at the court of Louis XVIII, for instance, had scarcely any connection with the Rivières, Blacas, d’Avarays, Vitrolles, d’Autichamps, Pasquiers, Larochejaqueleins, Decazes, Dambrays, Lainés, de Villèles, La Bourdonnayes, and others who shone at the court of Louis XV. Compare the courtiers of Henri IV with those of Louis XIV; you will hardly find five great families of the former time still in existence. The nephew of the great Richelieu was a very insignificant person at the court of Louis XIV; while His Majesty’s favorite, Villeroi, was the grandson of a secretary ennobled by Charles IX. And so it befell that the d’Esgrignons, all but princes under the Valois, and all-powerful in the time of Henri IV, had no fortune whatever at the court of Louis XVIII, which gave them not so much as a thought. At this day there are names as famous as those of royal houses﻿—the Foix-Graillys, for instance, or the d’Hérouvilles﻿—left to obscurity tantamount to extinction for want of money, the one power of the time.
All which things Victurnien beheld entirely from his own point of view; he felt the equality that he saw in Paris as a personal wrong. The monster Equality was swallowing down the last fragments of social distinction in the Restoration.

===Chinese===

这一次，这位法兰西贵族院议员正徒步在林荫大道上闲逛，手里撑一把伞，同任何一个普通人毫无二致；他连蓝绶带都没有戴——在从前，骑士团骑士不佩蓝绶带是不能公开露面的。德·勒农库公爵虽然贵为公爵、贵族院议员兼首席寝宫侍从，尽管礼数极其周到，读着亲戚的这封信仍禁不住微微一笑；这一笑告诉维克图尼安：古物陈列室与杜伊勒里宫之间，隔着的岂止六十里驿路；其间横亘着好几个世纪。
簇拥在王座周围的那一串家族的名字，一朝天子一朝臣，人物也随名字而变。宫廷这个天地里，同一出戏似乎每一代都重演一遍；可每一回登场的都是全然不同的一班人物。倘若不是历史证明事实如此，简直令人无法置信。譬如，路易十八宫廷里的显贵，同路易十五宫廷里煊赫的里维埃尔家、布拉卡家、德·阿瓦雷家、维特罗尔家、多蒂尚家、帕斯基耶家、拉罗什雅克兰家、德卡泽家、丹布雷家、莱内家、德·维莱尔家、拉布尔东内家等等，几乎没有多少渊源。把亨利四世的廷臣同路易十四的廷臣比一比；前一个时代还存在的世家，你几乎找不出五家。伟大的黎塞留的侄子，在路易十四的宫廷里不过是个无足轻重的人物；而陛下的宠臣维勒鲁瓦，却是一位被查理九世册封贵族的文书官的孙子。于是，德·埃斯格里尼翁家——瓦卢瓦王朝时几乎是亲王，亨利四世时代权倾一时——到了路易十八的宫廷，竟一点运势也没有了；宫廷连想也不想到他们。到今天，还有同王室一样显赫的姓氏——比如富瓦-格拉伊家，或德·埃鲁维尔家——因为缺钱，那个时代唯一的势力，被撇在无人知晓、形同灭绝的暗处。
这一切，维克图尼安全然是从自己的角度看取的；他把在巴黎见到的平等，感觉成对他个人的侮辱。平等这个怪物，正在吞噬复辟时期社会差别的最后残片。

===Original===

Having made up his mind on this head, he immediately proceeded to try to win back his place with such dangerous, if blunted weapons, as the age left to the noblesse. It is an expensive matter to gain the attention of Paris. To this end, Victurnien adopted some of the ways then in vogue. He felt that it was a necessity to have horses and fine carriages, and all the accessories of modern luxury; he felt, in short, “that a man must keep abreast of the times,” as de Marsay said﻿—de Marsay, the first dandy that he came across in the first drawing-room to which he was introduced. For his misfortune, he fell in with a set of roués, with de Marsay, de Ronquerolles, Maxime de Trailles, des Lupeaulx, Rastignac, Ajuda-Pinto, Beaudenord, de la Roche-Hugon, de Manerville, and the Vandenesses, whom he met wherever he went, and a great many houses were open to a young man with his ancient name and reputation for wealth. He went to the Marquise d’Espard’s, to the Duchesses de Grandlieu, de Carigliano, and de Chaulieu, to the Marquises d’Aiglemont and de Listomère, to Mme. de Sérizy’s, to the Opéra, to the embassies and elsewhere. The Faubourg Saint-Germain has its provincial genealogies at its fingers’ ends; a great name once recognized and adopted therein is a passport which opens many a door that will scarcely turn on its hinges for unknown names or the lions of a lower rank.
Victurnien found his relatives both amiable and ready to welcome him so long as he did not appear as a suppliant; he saw at once that the surest way of obtaining nothing was to ask for something. At Paris, if the first impulse moves people to protect, second thoughts (which last a good deal longer) impel them to despise the protégé. Independence, vanity, and pride, all the young Count’s better and worse feelings combined, led him, on the contrary, to assume an aggressive attitude. And therefore the Ducs de Verneuil, de Lenoncourt, de Chaulieu, de Navarreins, d’Hérouville, de Grandlieu, and de Maufrigneuse, the Princes de Cadignan and de Blamont-Chauvry, were delighted to present the charming survivor of the wreck of an ancient family at court.
Victurnien went to the Tuileries in a splendid carriage with his armorial bearings on the panels; but his presentation to His Majesty made it abundantly clear to him that the people occupied the royal mind so much that his nobility was like to be forgotten.

===Chinese===

在这一点上打定了主意，他随即着手用时代留给贵族的那些虽已钝拙、却依然危险的武器，去夺回自己的位置。要赢得巴黎的瞩目，是件花钱的事。为此，维克图尼安采纳了几种当时流行的做派。他觉得必须备下马匹和华丽的马车，以及现代奢侈品的全套行头；他觉得，简而言之，“人必须跟上时代”，正如德·玛赛所说——德·玛赛是他被引进的第一间客厅里遇到的第一个花花公子。他的不幸在于，他交上了一伙浪荡子：德·玛赛、德·龙克罗尔、马克西姆·德·特拉伊、德·吕波克斯、拉斯蒂涅、阿朱达-平托、博德诺尔、德·拉罗什-于贡、德·马纳维尔，还有汪德奈斯兄弟——走到哪里都碰见这些人；而凭着他的古老姓氏和富有之名，许多人家的大门都为这位青年敞开。他去德·埃斯帕尔侯爵夫人家，去德·格朗利厄、德·卡里利亚诺和德·肖利厄三位公爵夫人家，去德·艾格勒蒙和德·利斯托梅尔两位侯爵夫人家，去德·塞里齐夫人家，去歌剧院，去各处使馆，去各处应酬。圣日耳曼区对外省的家谱烂熟于心；一个显赫的姓氏一旦在那里得到承认和接纳，便是一份通行证，能敲开许多扇门——那些门对无名之辈或次一等的风头人物，是轻易不肯转动的。
亲戚们对维克图尼安都和蔼可亲，愿意接纳他，只要他不以哀求者的面目出现；他一眼看出，求人是最稳当的一无所获之法。在巴黎，第一阵冲动使人起意庇护，第二阵念头（持续得久得多）却使人鄙夷被庇护者。独立、虚荣与自尊，年轻伯爵或好或坏的全部情感拧在一起，反而把他推向进攻的姿态。于是，德·韦尔讷伊、德·勒农库、德·肖利厄、德·纳夫兰、德·埃鲁维尔、德·格朗利厄、德·莫弗里涅斯诸位公爵，德·卡迪尼昂和德·布拉蒙-绍弗里两位亲王，都乐于把这个古老门第沉船之后讨人喜欢的幸存者引荐到宫廷。
维克图尼安坐着饰有纹章的华丽马车去了杜伊勒里宫；可是陛下的接见叫他彻底明白：平民在御心里占了太大的位置，他的贵族身份很可能会被忘掉。

===Original===

The restored dynasty, moreover, was surrounded by triple ranks of eligible old men and gray-headed courtiers; the young noblesse was reduced to a cipher, and this Victurnien guessed at once. He saw that there was no suitable place for him at court, nor in the government, nor the army, nor, indeed, anywhere else. So he launched out into the world of pleasure. Introduced at the Élysée-Bourbon, at the Duchesse d’Angoulême’s, at the Pavillon Marsan, he met on all sides with the surface civilities due to the heir of an old family, not so old but it could be called to mind by the sight of a living member. And, after all, it was not a small thing to be remembered. In the distinction with which Victurnien was honored lay the way to the peerage and a splendid marriage; he had taken the field with a false appearance of wealth, and his vanity would not allow him to declare his real position. Besides, he had been so much complimented on the figure that he made, he was so pleased with his first success, that, like many other young men, he felt ashamed to draw back. He took a suite of rooms in the Rue du Bac, with stables and a complete equipment for the fashionable life to which he had committed himself. These preliminaries cost him fifty thousand francs, which money, moreover, the young gentleman managed to draw in spite of all Chesnel’s wise precautions, thanks to a series of unforeseen events.
Chesnel’s letter certainly reached his friend’s office, but Maître Sorbier was dead; and Mme. Sorbier, a matter-of-fact person, seeing it was a business letter, handed it on to her husband’s successor. Maître Cardot, the new notary, informed the young Count that a draft on the Treasury made payable to the deceased would be useless; and by way of reply to the letter, which had cost the old provincial notary so much thought, Cardot despatched four lines intended not to reach Chesnel’s heart, but to produce the money. Chesnel made the draft payable to Sorbier’s young successor; and the latter, feeling but little inclination to adopt his correspondent’s sentimentality, was delighted to put himself at the Count’s orders, and gave Victurnien as much money as he wanted.

===Chinese===

再说，复辟王朝周围里三层外三层围满了候缺的老臣和白发廷臣；年轻的贵族被压缩成一个零，这一点维克图尼安立刻就估量出来了。他看出，宫廷、政府、军队，乃至任何地方，都没有适合他的位置。于是他一头扎进了享乐世界。他被引进波旁宫爱丽舍厅、昂古莱姆公爵夫人的沙龙、马桑楼，处处受到一个古老门第继承人应得的表面礼遇——那门第虽古老，还要靠一个活着的后人才能唤起记忆。而被人记起，毕竟不是小事。维克图尼安所受的礼遇中，藏着通向贵族院和一门锦绣姻缘的路；他是摆出一副富有的假象出场的，虚荣心又不许他亮出真实处境。况且，他的排场受到那么多恭维，他对初战告捷如此得意，以至于像许多别的年轻人一样，缩回去太丢脸了。他在巴克街租下一套房间，带马厩，把他已投身其中的时髦生活所需的全套装备置办齐整。这番开局花掉他5万法郎——而且，多亏一连串始料未及的变故，这位青年先生竟然绕过了谢内尔的一切明智防范，把钱弄到了手。
谢内尔的信确实送到了他朋友的会计师事务所，可是索尔比耶师傅已经去世；索尔比耶太太是个讲实际的人，见是谈事务的信，便转给了丈夫的继任者。新公证人卡尔多师傅告诉年轻伯爵，付给亡者的国库汇票已无处兑付；作为对那封信的答复——老外省公证人为它熬了多少心思——卡尔多回了两行，图的不是打动谢内尔的心，而是把钱弄到手。谢内尔便把汇票改开给索尔比耶年轻的继任者；后者对来信人的多情善感毫无兴趣，乐得听伯爵差遣，维克图尼安要多少钱，他就给多少。

===Original===

Now those who know what life in Paris means, know that fifty thousand francs will not go very far in furniture, horses, carriages, and elegance generally; but it must be borne in mind that Victurnien immediately contracted some twenty thousand francs’ worth of debts besides, and his tradespeople at first were not at all anxious to be paid, for our young gentleman’s fortune had been prodigiously increased, partly by rumor, partly by Joséphin, that Chesnel in livery.
Victurnien had not been in town a month before he was obliged to repair to his man of business for ten thousand francs; he had only been playing whist with the Ducs de Navarreins, de Chaulieu, and de Lenoncourt, and now and again at his club. He had begun by winning some thousands of francs but pretty soon lost five or six thousand, which brought home to him the necessity of a purse for play. Victurnien had the spirit that gains goodwill everywhere, and puts a young man of a great family on a level with the very highest. He was not merely admitted at once into the band of patrician youth, but was even envied by the rest. It was intoxicating to him to feel that he was envied, nor was he in this mood very likely to think of reform. Indeed, he had completely lost his head. He would not think of the means; he dipped into his moneybags as if they could be refilled indefinitely; he deliberately shut his eyes to the inevitable results of the system. In that dissipated set, in the continual whirl of gaiety, people take the actors in their brilliant costumes as they find them; no one inquires whether a man can afford to make the figure he does, there is nothing in worse taste than inquiries as to ways and means. A man ought to renew his wealth perpetually, and as Nature does﻿—below the surface and out of sight. People talk if somebody comes to grief; they joke about a newcomer’s fortune till their minds are set at rest, and at this they draw the line. Victurnien d’Esgrignon, with all the Faubourg Saint-Germain to back him, with all his protectors exaggerating the amount of his fortune (were it only to rid themselves of responsibility), and magnifying his possessions in the most refined and well-bred way, with a hint or a word; with all these advantages﻿—to repeat﻿—Victurnien was, in fact, an eligible Count.

===Chinese===

深知巴黎生活是什么的人都清楚，5万法郎在家具、马匹、车辆和讲究的排场上撑不了多久；还得记住，维克图尼安随即又欠下了大约2万法郎的债，而商人们起初根本不急着讨账，因为我们这位青年先生的财产被大大地夸大了——一半靠流言，一半靠若瑟兰，那个穿号衣的谢内尔。
维克图尼安进城不出一个月，就不得不找他的事务先生支1万法郎；他不过同德·纳夫兰、德·肖利厄、德·勒农库几位公爵打惠斯特，偶尔也在俱乐部里玩玩。起初他赢了几千法郎，可不久便输掉五六千，这让他明白了赌本不可少。维克图尼安有那种到处赢得好感的气概，能把大世家的青年抬到同最高层平起平坐的地位。他不但立刻被贵胄青年的一伙接纳，甚至还遭人眼红。感到被人眼红，令他陶醉；处在这种心境，他不大可能想起改邪归正。确实，他完全昏了头。手段他一概不问；他把钱袋掏了又掏，仿佛那袋子取之不尽；对这套做派不可避免的结局，他存心闭眼不看。在那样放荡的圈子里，在无休止的寻欢作乐的旋转中，人们只认穿着光鲜行头的角色本身；没有谁去打听一个人撑不撑得起他的排场——再没有比追问经济来源更失趣味的事了。人应当像大自然那样不断更新自己的财富——在表层之下，在看不见的地方。有人栽了跟头，人们才议论；对新人家的财产，人们要取笑一番，直到自己放了心，也到此为止。维克图尼安·德·埃斯格里尼翁，背靠着整个圣日耳曼区，所有庇护人都在夸大他的家产（哪怕只是为了撇清自己的干系），以最文雅最有教养的方式，用一个暗示一句话替他抬高身价；有了这一切——我再说一遍——维克图尼安事实上是一位抢手的伯爵。

===Original===

He was handsome, witty, sound in politics; his father still possessed the ancestral castle and the lands of the marquisate. Such a young fellow is sure of an admirable reception in houses where there are marriageable daughters, fair but portionless partners at dances, and young married women who find that time hangs heavy on their hands. So the world, smiling, beckoned him to the foremost benches in its booth; the seats reserved for marquises are still in the same place in Paris; and if the names are changed, the things are the same as ever.
In the most exclusive circle of society in the Faubourg Saint-Germain, Victurnien found the Chevalier’s double in the person of the Vidame de Pamiers. The Vidame was a Chevalier de Valois raised to the tenth power, invested with all the prestige of wealth, enjoying all the advantages of high position. The dear Vidame was a repositary for everybody’s secrets, and the gazette of the Faubourg besides; nevertheless, he was discreet, and, like other gazettes, only said things that might safely be published. Again Victurnien listened to the Chevalier’s esoteric doctrines. The Vidame told young d’Esgrignon, without mincing matters, to make conquests among women of quality, supplementing the advice with anecdotes from his own experience. The Vicomte de Pamiers, it seemed, had permitted himself much that it would serve no purpose to relate here; so remote was it all from our modern manners, in which soul and passion play so large a part, that nobody would believe it. But the excellent Vidame did more than this.
“Dine with me at a tavern tomorrow,” said he, by way of conclusion. “We will digest our dinner at the Opéra, and afterwards I will take you to a house where several people have the greatest wish to meet you.”
The Vidame gave a delightful little dinner at the Rocher de Cancale; three guests only were asked to meet Victurnien﻿—de Marsay, Rastignac, and Blondet. Émile Blondet, the young Count’s fellow-townsman, was a man of letters on the outskirts of society to which he had been introduced by a charming woman from the same province. This was one of the Vicomte de Troisville’s daughters, now married to the Comte de Montcornet, one of those of Napoleon’s generals who went over to the Bourbons. The Vidame held that a dinner-party of more than six persons was beneath contempt.

===Chinese===

他英俊，机智，政治上立场纯正；他父亲还握有祖传的城堡和侯爵领地的田产。这样的青年，在有待嫁女儿的人家、舞会上美貌而无嫁妆的女伴中间、以及闲得发慌的少妇们中间，必定大受欢迎。于是，社交界笑吟吟地招呼他坐到戏棚里的前排；专为侯爵们保留的座位，在巴黎至今还在老地方；名字尽管换了，内容一如既往。
在圣日耳曼区最排外的社交圈子里，维克图尼安发现了骑士先生的化身——德·帕米耶总管。这位总管是十倍神通的德瓦卢瓦骑士，坐拥财富的全部声威，占尽高位的一切便宜。亲爱的总管是人人秘密的保管库，又是本区的活报纸；不过他口风很紧，同别的报纸一样，只说那些登出来也保险的话。维克图尼安又一次听起了骑士先生的秘传教义。总管直截了当地教导小德·埃斯格里尼翁，要他在上流妇人中间攻城掠地，还以自己的亲身经历作注脚。这位帕米耶子爵看来干过的事，写出来毫无益处；那一切同我们的现代风俗——灵魂与激情在其中占着那么大的比重——相距太远，说了也没有人信。可是这位出色的总管还不止于此。
“明天同我去小饭馆吃顿饭，”他末了说道，“吃完上歌剧院消食，然后我带你去一户人家，那里有几个人极想见你。”
总管在康卡尔岩饭馆设了一席妙不可言的小宴；专为陪维克图尼安，只请了三位客人——德·玛赛、拉斯蒂涅和布隆代。埃米尔·布隆代是年轻伯爵的小同乡，一个文人，徘徊在社交界的边缘——把他引进这个圈子的是一位同乡的可爱女人，即德·特鲁瓦维尔子爵的一个女儿，如今嫁给了蒙科内伯爵——拿破仑麾下倒向波庸王朝的将军之一。总管认为，六个人以上的宴席不屑一顾。

===Original===

In that case, according to him, there was an end alike of cookery and conversation, and a man could not sip his wine in a proper frame of mind.
“I have not yet told you, my dear boy, where I mean to take you tonight,” he said, taking Victurnien’s hands and tapping on them. “You are going to see Mlle. des Touches; all the pretty women with any pretensions to wit will be at her house *en petit comité*. Literature, art, poetry, any sort of genius, in short, is held in great esteem there. It is one of our old-world *bureaux d’esprit*, with a veneer of monarchical doctrine, the livery of this present age.”
“It is sometimes as tiresome and tedious there as a pair of new boots, but there are women with whom you cannot meet anywhere else,” said de Marsay.
“If all the poets who went there to rub up their muse were like our friend here,” said Rastignac, tapping Blondet familiarly on the shoulder, “we should have some fun. But a plague of odes, and ballads, and driveling meditations, and novels with wide margins, pervades the sofas and the atmosphere.”
“I don’t dislike them,” said de Marsay, “so long as they corrupt girls’ minds, and don’t spoil women.”
“Gentlemen,” smiled Blondet, “you are encroaching on my field of literature.”
“You need not talk. You have robbed us of the most charming woman in the world, you lucky rogue; we may be allowed to steal your less brilliant ideas,” cried Rastignac.
“Yes, he is a lucky rascal,” said the Vidame, and he twitched Blondet’s ear. “But perhaps Victurnien here will be luckier still this evening﻿—”
“*Already!*” exclaimed de Marsay. “Why, he only came here a month ago; he has scarcely had time to shake the dust of his old manor house off his feet, to wipe off the brine in which his aunt kept him preserved; he has only just set up a decent horse, a tilbury in the latest style, a groom﻿—”
“No, no, not a groom,” interrupted Rastignac; “he has some sort of an agricultural laborer that he brought with him ‘from his place.’ Buisson, who understands a livery as well as most, declared that the man was physically incapable of wearing a jacket.”
“I will tell you what, you ought to have modeled yourself on Beaudenord,” the Vidame said seriously. “He has this advantage over all of you, my young friends, he has a genuine specimen of the English tiger﻿—”
“Just see, gentlemen, what the noblesse have come to in France!” cried Victurnien.

===Chinese===

他说，人一多，烹饪和谈话便同归于尽，人喝酒也喝不出正当的心境。
“我还没告诉你呢，亲爱的孩子，今晚我要把你带到哪里去，”他说着，握起维克图尼安的双手，轻轻拍着。“你要去见德·图什小姐；凡自命风雅的漂亮女人，都会在小圈子里聚在她家。文学、艺术、诗歌，总之任何一种天才，在那里都极受尊崇。那是我们旧世界的一个才智沙龙，外加一层君主主义学说的漆皮——本时代的号衣。”
“那地方有时腻烦得像一双新靴子，可那里有几位女人，你在别处休想见到。”德·玛赛说。
“要是去那里给缪斯上油的诗人都像我们这位朋友，”拉斯蒂涅拍着布隆代的肩膀，熟不拘礼地说，“我们倒有的乐了。可是什么颂歌啦、谣曲啦、流口水的沉思录啦、天地留白的小说啦，乌烟瘴气，弥漫在沙发和空气里。”
“我倒不讨厌那些东西，”德·玛赛说，“只要它们带坏的是姑娘的脑子，别糟蹋了妇人。”
“诸位，”布隆代笑道，“你们这是侵入我的文学领地了。”
“你还说呢。你把世上最迷人的女人从我们手里抢走了，你这个走运的无赖；那我们偷你几个不那么精彩的念头，总还使得。”拉斯蒂涅嚷道。
“不错，他是个走运的坏小子。”总管说着，拧了拧布隆代的耳朵。“不过今晚也许我们这位维克图尼安更有艳福——”
“这就开始了！”德·玛赛嚷道，“嗬，他来这儿才一个月；还没来得及抖掉老庄园鞋上的尘土，还没洗掉他姑妈拿盐把他腌着的那层卤水；他刚置下一匹像样的马、一辆最新式的双轮轻马车、一个马夫——”
“不，不，不是马夫，”拉斯蒂涅打断他，“他从‘他家庄子上’带来的是一个庄稼汉之类的东西。比松——论号衣的学问他不输给任何人——断言那人天生穿不进号衣。”
“我说你们呀，都该拿博德诺尔做榜样，”总管一本正经地说，“他比你们大家强的地方，我的年轻朋友们，就在于他有一件地道样品：英国的‘老虎’——”
“诸位请看，法国的贵族都沦落到什么地步了！”维克图尼安喊道。

===Original===

“For them the one important thing is to have a tiger, a thoroughbred, and baubles﻿—”
“Bless me!” said Blondet. “ ‘This gentleman’s good sense at times appalls me.’﻿—Well, yes, young moralist, you nobles have come to that. You have not even left to you that lustre of lavish expenditure for which the dear Vidame was famous fifty years ago. We revel on a second floor in the Rue Montorgueil. There are no more wars with the Cardinal, no Field of the Cloth of Gold. You, Comte d’Esgrignon, in short, are supping in the company of one Blondet, younger son of a miserable provincial magistrate, with whom you would not shake hands down yonder; and in ten years’ time you may sit beside him among peers of the realm. Believe in yourself after that, if you can.”
“Ah, well,” said Rastignac, “we have passed from action to thought, from brute force to force of intellect, we are talking﻿—”
“Let us not talk of our reverses,” protested the Vidame; “I have made up my mind to die merrily. If our friend here has not a tiger as yet, he comes of a race of lions, and can dispense with one.”
“He cannot do without a tiger,” said Blondet; “he is too newly come to town.”
“His elegance may be new as yet,” returned de Marsay, “but we are adopting it. He is worthy of us, he understands his age, he has brains, he is nobly born and gently bred; we are going to like him, and serve him, and push him﻿—”
“Whither?” inquired Blondet.
“Inquisitive soul!” said Rastignac.
“With whom will he take up tonight?” de Marsay asked.
“With a whole seraglio,” said the Vidame.
“Plague take it! What can we have done that the dear Vidame is punishing us by keeping his word to the infanta? I should be pitiable indeed if I did not know her﻿—”
“And I was once a coxcomb even as he,” said the Vidame, indicating de Marsay.
The conversation continued pitched in the same key, charmingly scandalous, and agreeably corrupt. The dinner went off very pleasantly. Rastignac and de Marsay went to the Opéra with the Vidame and Victurnien, with a view to following them afterwards to Mlle. des Touches’ salon. And thither, accordingly, this pair of rakes betook themselves, calculating that by that time the tragedy would have been read; for of all things to be taken between eleven and twelve o’clock at night, a tragedy in their opinion was the most unwholesome.

===Chinese===

“对他们来说，头等要紧的事就是有一个‘老虎’、一匹纯种马、几件金晃晃的小玩意——”
“天哪！”布隆代说，“‘这位先生的神智清明，有时真叫我骇怕。’——是啊，年轻的道学家，你们贵族就落到这一步了。连那种挥金如土的光彩——五十年前亲爱的总管就以此闻名——也没有留给你们。我们在蒙托格伊街的三层楼上狂欢。同红衣主教的战争没有了，锦绣营地也没有了。总之，你，德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵，此刻正陪一个布隆代吃宵夜——一个外省穷法官的小儿子，在你们那边你都不会同他握手；而十年之后，你也许会坐在他身旁，同列王国贵族院。此后你若还信得过自己，你就信吧。”
“唉，是啊，”拉斯蒂涅说，“我们已经从行动转入思想，从蛮力转入智力，我们现在谈的是——”
“别谈我们的败运了，”总管抗议道，“我拿定主意要快快活活地死。我们这位朋友眼下虽还没有‘老虎’，他可是狮子的种族，没有也过得去。”
“他非有‘老虎’不可，”布隆代说，“他进城的日子还太新。”
“他的风雅也许还新鲜，”德·玛赛答道，“可我们正在领养它。他配得上我们，他懂得他的时代，他有头脑，出身高贵，教养良好；我们会喜欢他，为他效劳，推他一把——”
“推向哪里？”布隆代问。
“好奇心真重！”拉斯蒂涅说。
“他今晚会同谁搭上？”德·玛赛问。
“同整整一座后宫。”总管说。
“见鬼！我们造了什么孽，亲爱的总管居然拿遵守对公主的诺言来惩罚我们？我要是不认识她，那才可怜呢——”
“我当年也曾是个花花公子，同他一模一样。”总管说着，指了指德·玛赛。
谈话一直保持在这个调门上，风雅地伤风败俗，愉快地腐化堕落。这顿饭吃得非常尽兴。拉斯蒂涅和德·玛赛同总管、维克图尼安一起去了歌剧院，打算随后跟到德·图什小姐的沙龙。于是这一对浪子如约前往，掐算着到那时悲剧已经念完了；因为照他们的看法，夜里十一点到十二点之间下肚的东西里，要数悲剧最伤身体。

===Original===

They went to keep a watch on Victurnien and to embarrass him, a piece of schoolboys’s mischief embittered by a jealous dandy’s spite. But Victurnien was gifted with that page’s effrontery which is a great help to ease of manner; and Rastignac, watching him as he made his entrance, was surprised to see how quickly he caught the tone of the moment.
“That young d’Esgrignon will go far, will he not?” he said, addressing his companion.
“That is as may be,” returned de Marsay, “but he is in a fair way.”
---
The Vidame introduced his young friend to one of the most amiable and frivolous duchesses of the day, a lady whose adventures caused an explosion five years later. Just then, however, she was in the full blaze of her glory; she had been suspected, it is true, of equivocal conduct; but suspicion, while it is still suspicion and not proof, marks a woman out with the kind of distinction which slander gives to a man. Nonentities are never slandered; they chafe because they are left in peace. This woman was, in fact, the Duchesse de Maufrigneuse, a daughter of the d’Uxelles; her father-in-law was still alive; she was not to be the Princesse de Cadignan for some years to come. A friend of the Duchesse de Langeais and the Vicomtesse de Bauséant, two glories departed, she was likewise intimate with the Marquise d’Espard, with whom she disputed her fragile sovereignty as queen of fashion. Great relations lent her countenance for a long while, but the Duchesse de Maufrigneuse was one of those women who, in some way, nobody knows how, or why, or where, will spend the rents of all the lands of earth, and of the moon likewise, if they were not out of reach. The general outline of her character was scarcely known as yet; de Marsay, and de Marsay only, really had read her. That redoubtable dandy now watched the Vidame de Pamiers’ introduction of his young friend to that lovely woman, and bent over to say in Rastignac’s ear:
“My dear fellow, he will go up *whizz!* like a rocket, and come down like a stick,” an atrociously vulgar saying which was remarkably fulfilled.
The Duchesse de Maufrigneuse had lost her heart to Victurnien after first giving her mind to a serious study of him. Any lover who should have caught the glance by which she expressed her gratitude to the Vidame might well have been jealous of such friendship.

===Chinese===

他们去是为了盯住维克图尼安、让他难堪，一场学童式的恶作剧，掺着花花公子的醋意和毒心。可是维克图尼安天生有侍童那种厚脸皮，这对举止潇洒是大有好处的；拉斯蒂涅看着他走进场子，惊讶地发现他多快就抓住了当日的调门。
“这个小德·埃斯格里尼翁会有出息的，是不是？”他对同伴说。
“那也难说，”德·玛赛答道，“不过他眼下正一路顺风。”
---
总管把他的年轻朋友引荐给当日最可爱也最轻佻的一位公爵夫人——五年之后，这位夫人的艳闻掀起过一场轩然大波。不过此时，她正当荣光鼎盛；诚然，她的操守受过怀疑；可怀疑只要还停留在怀疑而没成为证据，就会给女人标出一种特殊的身价，正如诽谤之于男人。无名之辈从来没人诽谤；他们还苦恼呢，苦恼人家放着他们不管。这位夫人便是德·莫弗里涅斯公爵夫人，德·于克塞尔家的小姐；她的公公还在世；离她成为德·卡迪尼昂亲王夫人还有些年头。她是德·朗热公爵夫人和德·鲍赛昂子爵夫人——两朵已然凋零的名花——的朋友，又同德·埃斯帕尔侯爵夫人过从甚密，同她争夺时麾女王的脆弱宝座。显赫的姻亲长期给她撑腰；可是德·莫弗里涅斯公爵夫人属于那样一类女人：不知怎么地、不知为什么、也不知在哪里，她们能把地上一切田产的租金都花光，连月球的田产，只要够得着，也一并花光。她性格的总体轮廓当时还鲜为人知；德·玛赛，而且只有德·玛赛，真正读懂了她。此刻，这位令人生畏的花花公子眼看德·帕米耶总管把他的年轻朋友引荐给那位可爱的夫人，便俯身在拉斯蒂涅耳边说：
“亲爱的，他会‘嗖’的一声像火箭一样窜上去，然后像根棍子一样掉下来。”——一句糙得吓人的俗话，后来应验得出奇地准。
德·莫弗里涅斯公爵夫人先用心把维克图尼安认真研究了一番，然后才把心交给他。哪个情人若撞见她向总管表示感谢的那道目光，完全有理由嫉妒这样的友谊。

===Original===

Women are like horses let loose on a steppe when they feel, as the Duchess felt with the Vidame de Pamiers, that the ground is safe; at such moments they are themselves; perhaps it pleases them to give, as it were, samples of their tenderness in intimacy in this way. It was a guarded glance, nothing was lost between eye and eye; there was no possibility of reflection in any mirror. Nobody intercepted it.
“See how she has prepared herself,” Rastignac said, turning to de Marsay. “What a virginal toilette; what swan’s grace in that snow-white throat of hers! How white her gown is, and she is wearing a sash like a little girl; she looks round like a madonna inviolate. Who would think that you had passed that way?”
“The very reason why she looks as she does,” returned de Marsay, with a triumphant air.
The two young men exchanged a smile. Mme. de Maufrigneuse saw the smile and guessed at their conversation, and gave the pair a broadside of her eyes, an art acquired by Frenchwomen since the Peace, when Englishwomen imported it into this country, together with the shape of their silver plate, their horses and harness, and the piles of insular ice which impart a refreshing coolness to the atmosphere of any room in which a certain number of British females are gathered together. The young men grew serious as a couple of clerks at the end of a homily from headquarters before the receipt of an expected bonus.
The Duchess when she lost her heart to Victurnien had made up her mind to play the part of romantic Innocence, a role much understudied subsequently by other women, for the misfortune of modern youth. Her Grace of Maufrigneuse had just come out as an angel at a moment’s notice, precisely as she meant to turn to literature and science somewhere about her fortieth year instead of taking to devotion. She made a point of being like nobody else. Her parts, her dresses, her caps, opinions, toilettes, and manner of acting were all entirely new and original. Soon after her marriage, when she was scarcely more than a girl, she had played the part of a knowing and almost depraved woman; she ventured on risky repartees with shallow people, and betrayed her ignorance to those who knew better.

===Chinese===

当女人们觉得脚下安全时——就像公爵夫人同德·帕米耶总管相处时那样——她们就像放归草原的马；这样的时刻，她们才是她们自己；也许她们乐意这样半露半藏地展一展亲昵的温柔，权当样品。那是一道有掩遮的目光，眼与眼之间滴水不漏；任何镜子里都不可能留下映象。没有人截获它。
“瞧她把自己拾掇的，”拉斯蒂涅转向德·玛赛说，“多么贞洁的打扮；她那雪白的颈项，多么有天鹅的风姿！她的长裙多白，腰上还系着小姑娘式的腰带；她环顾四周的样子，像一尊未受侵犯的圣母。谁想得到你曾从那条路上走过？”
“这正是她所以如此打扮的缘故。”德·玛赛带着得胜的神气回答。
两个年轻人相视一笑。德·莫弗里涅斯夫人看见了这一笑，猜到了他们谈的内容，便拿眼睛朝这一对齐射了一轮排炮——这门技艺是和议以来法国女人学会的，那时英国女人把它带进法国，连同她们银餐具的样式、马匹和马具，还有那一堆堆海岛冰块——只要有相当数目的英国女人聚在一处，那些冰块便给房里的空气送来沁人的凉意。两位年轻人顿时严肃起来，像两个职员听总部训完话、正等着发放盼来的花红。
公爵夫人把心交给维克图尼安时，已打定主意扮演浪漫的“天真”这个角色——后来被别的女人大量研习扮演，成为现代青年的不幸。莫弗里涅斯公爵夫人是说变就变，转眼间以天使面目出场，正如她打定主意到四十岁上下不去信教，而去投身文学和科学一样。她刻意与众不同。她的角色、她的裙衫、她的帽子、她的见解、她的打扮和做派，全是崭新的、独创的。婚后不久，她还几乎是个姑娘的时候，她扮演过精明世故、近乎堕落的女人；她同浅薄之徒斗胆说些出格的俏皮话，又在高明人面前露了自己的无知。

===Original===

As the date of that marriage made it impossible to abstract one little year from her age without the knowledge of Time, and as Her Grace had reached her twenty-sixth year, she had taken it into her head to be immaculate. She scarcely seemed to belong to earth; she shook out her wide sleeves as if they had been wings. Her eyes fled to heaven at too warm a glance, or word, or thought.
